Declarative Linearizability Proofs for Descriptor-Based Concurrent Helping Algorithms
Jesús Domínguez, Aleksandar Nanevski

Abstract
Linearizability is a standard correctness criterion for concurrent algorithms, typically proved by establishing the algorithms’ linearization points. However, relying on linearization points leads to proofs that are implementation-dependent, and thus hinder abstraction and reuse. In this paper we show that one can develop more declarative proofs by foregoing linearization points and instead relying on a technique of axiomatization of visibility relations. While visibility relations have been considered before, ours is the first study where the challenge is to formalize the helping nature of the algorithms. In particular, we show that by axiomatizing the properties of separation between events that contain bunches of help requests, we can extract what is common for high-level understanding of several descriptor-based helping algorithms of Harris et al. (RDCSS, MCAS, and optimizations), and produce novel proofs of their linearizability that share significant components.



1
Introduction

Helping
is a design principle for concurrent algorithms in
which a process carries out work for other processes.
Helping is useful for achieving lock-free and wait-free
implementations, because whenever a process
p
becomes stuck, other
processes can help
p
make progress, or
p
itself can help the
process that is in its way, so that
p
can
continue
[
5
,
4
,
2
]
.
Helping algorithms are often equipped with
descriptors
, which
are structures that a process uses to signal that it requests help
with its task. Whenever a process
p
finds a descriptor in some
pointer
x
, it means that some other process
q
has requested help
with computing the value of
x
. Process
p
then extracts
q
’s task
information from the descriptor and
p

fully
helps until
q
’s
task terminates; only then,
p
attempts its own task.

In this paper, we focus on proving correct two descriptor-based helping algorithms:
RDCSS and MCAS
[
11
]
.
Restricted Double-Compare Single Swap (RDCSS)
is
a generalization of the compare-and-swap operation CAS. Whereas

\textit{CAS}\,(pt_{2},exp_{2},new_{2})
updates
pt_{2}
with
new_{2}
if the
old value of
pt_{2}
is
exp_{2}
, RDCSS adds another pointer
pt_{1}
and
value
exp_{1}
into the decision. More precisely, RDCSS receives as
input a descriptor that is a record with five pieces of data: two
pointers
pt_{1}
,
pt_{2}
and three values
exp_{1}
,
exp_{2}
,
new_{2}
.
To the invoking client, RDCSS gives the impression that it

atomically
carries out the following update: if
pt_{1}
has
expected value
exp_{1}
and
pt_{2}
has expected value
exp_{2}
, then

pt_{2}
is updated to
new_{2}
.

Multiple Compare-And-Swap (MCAS)
also generalizes
CAS, but it updates an arbitrary number of pointers at once.
More precisely, MCAS receives a list of update entries, each being a
record with three pieces of data: a pointer
pt
and two
values
exp
,
new
. To a client, MCAS gives the
impression that it atomically carries out the following conditional
multiple update: if for every update entry
i
, the pointer

{\textit{pt}}_{i}
has expected value

{\textit{exp}}_{i}
, then for every update entry
j
, the
pointer
{\textit{pt}}_{j}
is updated to the new value

{\textit{new}}_{j}
.

These algorithms have recently become somewhat of a verification
benchmark
[
21
,
15
,
18
]
due to their tricky

linearizability
1
1

1

A structure is
linearizable

[
14
]
if in every
concurrent execution history of the structure’s exportable methods,
the method invocations can be ordered linearly just by permuting
overlapping invocations, so that the obtained history is

sequentially sound
; that is, executing the methods
sequentially in the linear order produces the same outputs that the
methods had in the concurrent history. In other words, every
concurrent history is equivalent to a sequential one where methods
execute without interference, i.e.,
atomically
. The linear
order must include all terminated methods, but may also include
selected non-terminated ones, if their partial execution influenced
(i.e., was
visible
to)
others.
proofs.
The existing linearizability proofs of RDCSS and MCAS all employ the

linearization point
(LP) approach. Given a method (henceforth,

event
), its LP is the moment in the event’s duration at which the
event’s effect can be considered to have occurred
abstractly
,
in the sense that the linearization order of the events is determined
by the real-time order of the chosen LPs. The LPs are always
described
operationally
, by indicating a line in the code
together with a run-time condition under which the line applies (thus,
the code line chosen as the LP may vary). The linearizability proof
then shows that the effect of the invocation abstractly occurs at the
declared line.
The operational nature of the LP description leads to proofs that are
implementation-dependent. Concretely, RDCSS and MCAS
are operationally tricky because, in addition to helping, they exhibit
so called “future-dependent LPs”. These are LPs whose position
depends on a run-time condition in the future, potentially even after
the considered event has terminated.

In this paper, we develop a novel approach to proving linearizability
of RDCSS and MCAS (and optimizations) that elides LPs altogether. Our
approach is
declarative
in nature, rather than operational. In
particular, we show that RDCSS and MCAS can be explained in an
implementation-independent manner that
brings to light the key
high-level abstract design principles that they both share
. This in
turn leads to abstract proofs of linearizability that reuse
significant portions of the formal development.

More specifically, our first contribution is proposing that the kind
of helping employed by RDCSS and MCAS induces two natural notions that
can be used to capture the essence of both algorithms.
The first notion is that of
separation
between events. Since an
event that finds a descriptor carries out its task only after
completing the task of the event that requested help, there is a

gap
in real time between tasks carried out by the two events.
This behavior also induces the effect that help requests on the same
pointer are
disjoint
in time, since a process must help first
(hence, cannot make a help request) if there is a descriptor present.
In addition, RDCSS and MCAS collect their help requests into
“bunches” so that the bunched help requests of one event can easily
be separated from the bunched help requests of another event.
The second notion is that of
observation
. It arises because
RDCSS and MCAS modules contain procedures that modify the state, and
procedures that read the state. Thus, a reader event
A
may observe a
value written by writer event
B
, leading to a dependence of
A
’s
result on
B
.
In a sequentially sound reordering of events, no event sequenced
between
B
and
A
may overwrite
B
’s write.

Our second contribution is
axiomatizing
separation and
observation,
which formally exposes the common semantic structure behind RDCSS and
MCAS and makes it possible to decompose their proofs into independent
components. As common in axiomatic systems, a proof of a property
developed out of axioms applies without change to any algorithm that
satisfies the axioms.
We thus proceed to prove linearizability of RDCSS and
MCAS out of the axioms about separation and observation alone. The axioms are much
easier to establish and understand than attempting the linearizability
proof for either of the algorithms from scratch and in its
totality.
Certain aspects of the abstract linearizability proof (e.g., existence
of a linear order) are common to MCAS and RDCSS, while other aspects
(e.g., sequential soundness) are unique to each data structure, but
are still abstract in the sense that the proof can be reused by other
implementations.

To state our axioms, we encode the notions of separation and
observation as binary relations between events. Each relation
describes a different notion of dependence between events. The
axioms then state properties or behaviors expected to hold whenever
these order dependences are present. A prior event on which a latter
event may depend, is said to be “visible” to the latter event,
lending the name of
visibility relations

[
22
]
to our
notions of separation and observation.
Visibility relations are clearly relevant to linearizability, where a
notion of one event influencing others is crucial, as evident from our
description of linearizability in
Footnote
1
.

Axiomatizing the visibility between events via binary relations was
first proposed as an alternative to the LP approach by
Henzinger et al.
[
13
]
, who applied visibility to axiomatize
concurrent queues, and termed “aspects” the various properties that
implied the axiomatization.
Subsequently, axioms for event visibility were developed for
the concurrent timestamped stack
[
7
]
, for sophisticated
concurrent snapshot algorithms
[
19
]
, and to formalize
relaxations of linearizability and
of memory consistency
[
8
,
20
]
.
Our paper, however, is the first to apply visibility relations to a study
of helping algorithms, which poses very different challenges. In
particular, whereas axioms for concurrent queues, stacks, and
snapshots mostly capture how the contents of the structure evolve
extensionally (e.g., queues are FIFO, stacks are LIFO, etc.), with
RDCSS and MCAS the challenge is in describing intensional aspects of
event interaction, such as temporal bunching of help requests and the
resulting separation of events.
In summary, our contributions are:

•

We propose separation and observation as foundational notions to
formalize the properties of RDCSS and MCAS algorithms in a
declarative style.

•

We provide the first axiomatization of these two algorithms (or
of any helping algorithm) in the style of visibility relations
(Section
3.2
).
The axiomatization captures the
common semantic structure underpinning the two algorithms, and
substantiates that visibility relations are a powerful method for
verification of concurrent algorithms. We show that separation and
observation axioms (henceforth, visibility axioms) imply
linearizability (Section
4
).

•

To relate separation, observation and descriptors, we formulate another
set of axioms, called span axioms
(Section
5.2
).
These mathematically describe, in implementation-independent way,
how descriptors in RDCSS and MCAS collect the help requests into
disjoint bunches. We prove that the span axioms imply the visibility
axioms (Section
5.2
). Thus, linearizability is
further reduced to establishing the span axioms, allowing for even
more proof reuse.

•

We show that RDCSS and MCAS satisfy the span axioms, concluding
that both algorithms are linearizable
(Section
5.3
for MCAS,
Appendix
C.1
for
RDCSS).

•

We also show that separation and observation generalize beyond
helping. In particular, we consider
(Section
2.2.1
) an optimization of the
read procedure in MCAS, introduced by Harris et al.
[
11
]
, which we call
“opportunistic reading”. Opportunistic readers do not help on a
descriptor; they merely determine their return value from the
descriptor, but improve their own efficiency by leaving the actual
helping to others (proofs in Appendix
D
).

1
summarizes the structure of our
linearizability proof and of our paper. Solid boxes and lines
indicate what we discuss in the main body of the paper; these include
common considerations, and detailed treatment of the proof of
MCAS. Dashed boxes and lines indicate the material about RDCSS, MCAS
with opportunistic readers, and the generalization of the span axioms
that deal with opportunism, found in the
appendices.

in the appendices.

C-like pseudo code of the RDCSS implementation (essential fragment).
!
,

Alloc
and
CAS
are the system calls for memory
dereference, allocation, and compare-and-swap.

Figure
2
shows the
implementation of the RDCSS data structure (
rdcss
and associated
methods, and the descriptor type
rdesc
).
Val
denotes the
set of all possible input values; it excludes descriptors and
descriptor-storing pointers.
Every pointer exposed to the clients is classified as a control
(
cptr
) or data (
dptr
) pointer. Data pointers may
store
Val
values, descriptors, and pointers, while control
pointers only store
Val
values. Along with the
rdcss

method, the implementation exports data pointer methods

rread
,
rwrite
, and
rCAS
. The latter
replace the system calls for pointer dereference, update, and

CAS
, which they adapt to the presence of control and data
pointers. Not shown, but also exported, are the methods for
dereference, update and
CAS
over control pointers,
implemented simply as respective system calls. Methods
\textit{rdcss}^{\prime}

and
complete
are internal, and not exported.
The implementation further assumes: (1) an exported method

\textit{ralloc}\,(v,t)
for allocating a pointer of type

t\in\{\textsc{cptr},\textsc{dptr}\}
with initial value
v
, which replaces
the system operation
Alloc
, and (2) an internal Boolean
predicate
\textit{is\_rdesc}\,(p)
that returns true iff pointer
p

stores an RDCSS descriptor. We show their implementation in
Appendix
C.1
, but elide here as it
is not essential for understanding.

We next give a high-level description of
rdcss
. Any thread
T

invoking
rdcss
allocates a fresh address
d
for the input
descriptor
desc
(line
7
);
d
will
serve as a unique identifier for the
rdcss
invocation. Next,
T

calls the recursive procedure
\textit{rdcss}^{\prime}
. This first CASs on

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
to read the
old
value and write

d
if the old value is the expected
{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}

(line
11
). By this write,
T
essentially requests
help with
d
, and signals to other threads that:

•

Descriptor
desc
(which is stored in
d
) is currently
active in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
.

•

T
is invoking
rdcss
in which pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{1}
still needs to be read.

•

While
desc
remains active in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
, value

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
is stored in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}

indirectly
, as it can be reached by following the descriptor.

After the CAS in line
11
, if
old
is a
descriptor, then
T
first
helps
by invoking

\textit{complete}{(\textit{old})}

(line
13
), and then
T
recurses to
reattempt its task. If
old
is a value not matching

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
, then no modification to

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
is performed and
T

returns
old
. If
old
is the expected value (i.e.,
the CAS succeeded), then
T
invokes
\textit{complete}{(d)}
to help itself
(line
17
).

We describe
complete
from the point of view of another thread

T^{\prime}
that reads
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
and finds
d
(lines

12
,
29
,

37
,
45
). Before
doing anything else,
T^{\prime}
helps on
d
by invoking
complete
as
follows (lines
13
,

30
,

38
,

46
).
T^{\prime}
first reads pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{1}
(line
22
). If it
finds the expected value
{\textit{desc}}.exp_{1}
, and since pointer

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
indirectly stores the expected value, then

rdcss
can succeed; thus,
T^{\prime}
attempts to replace the descriptor
in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
with the new value

{\textit{desc}}.new_{2}
(line
24
). The
replacement in line
24
is performed by

CAS
, which may fail if some other thread managed to help
T

before
T^{\prime}
. Either way,
T
has been helped after the call to

complete
.
Otherwise, if
T^{\prime}
does not find the expected value

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{1}
, it attempts to undo
T
’s modification to

{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
(line
26
) by
replacing
d
in
{\textit{desc}}.pt_{2}
back to

{\textit{desc}}.exp_{2}
, via
CAS
. This undoing may fail if
some other thread managed to help
T
before
T^{\prime}
. Either way,
T

has again been helped after the call to
complete
.

Procedures
rread
,
rCAS
and
rwrite
follow
the same helping strategy, i.e., whenever they find a descriptor in
their input pointer, they invoke
complete
to help the pending

rdcss
before recursing to reattempt their own task.

Figure 3
:
C-like pseudo code of the MCAS implementation (essential fragment). The command
return
finishes the procedure execution where it is used.

Figure
3
shows the implementation of the MCAS data structure
(
mcas
and associated methods, and the descriptor type

mdesc
) atop RDCSS.
Procedures
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
and
writeall
are internal. The
figure elides the exported procedures
mread
,
mwrite
,
and
malloc
, which can be found in
Appendix
C.2
.
Again,
Val
is the set of input values that excludes descriptors
and descriptor-storing pointers.
We use the notation
{pt}_{i}
,
{exp}_{i}
, and

{new}_{i}
to refer to the components of an
update_entry

i
.

High-level description of
mcas
is as follows. Thread
T

invoking
mcas
first creates an MCAS descriptor
desc

containing the non-empty list of update entries and a status pointer
(line
23
). The status pointer starts in an
undecided state (
Undec
) and evolves into either a success
(
Succ
) or failed (
Fail
) state.
Succ
indicates
that the descriptor was written into all input pointers, while

Fail
indicates the failure of at least one such write. Next,
T

allocates a fresh address
d
for
desc

(line
24
), which serves as the unique identifier
for the
mcas
invocation. Eventually, through a call to

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
,
T
invokes
writeall

(line
13
) to attempt storing
d
into each
input pointer, via
rdcss
. If any individual
rdcss
of

writeall
fails, then
T
attempts to mark the status
pointer of
desc
as failed (line
14
). In
general,
T
writing
d
into pointer
{pt}_{i}
of entry
i

signals that writing
d
into previous entries has succeeded and:

•

Descriptor
desc
(stored in
d
) is currently active
in
{pt}_{j}
for every
j\leq i
.

•

T
has an ongoing
mcas
in which pointers

{pt}_{j}
for
j>i
still need to be updated with
d
.

•

Pointers
{pt}_{j}
for
j\leq i
indirectly have
their expected values.

If another thread
T^{\prime}
attempts to access
{pt}_{i}
and finds

d
, it will
help

T
complete
mcas
before doing anything
else, by invoking
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
as follows.
T^{\prime}
first attempts to
write the descriptor into all remaining pointers
(line
13
). If
T^{\prime}
succeeds, then all
pointers had the expected values. The variable
s
in line

13
is set to

Succ
, and
T^{\prime}
attempts changing the status field of
desc

accordingly (line
14
) and replacing all the
descriptors with the new values (line
18
). These
attempts utilize
CAS
which may fail if some other thread
already helped
T
to either succeed or fail in its
mcas
.
Alternatively, if
T^{\prime}
fails to write
d
into some pointer, it means
that not all pointers had the expected values. The variable
s
in
line
13
is bound to
Fail
,
T^{\prime}
attempts to change the status field
of
desc
accordingly (line
14
), and to undo

T
’s writing of
d

(line
18
). As
before, the
CAS
’s in these attempts may fail if some other
thread already helped
T
to either succeed or fail in its
mcas
.

Notice that
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
and
writeall
are mutually
recursive. This is necessary, because while a thread
T
is writing
the descriptor into all the input pointers using
writeall
,

T
may encounter other descriptors that force it to help by invoking

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
(lines

32
-
34
),
after which
T
must reattempt writing the descriptor again
(line
35
).

2.2.1
Helping reading vs. opportunistic reading

1:

proc

mread
(
pt:\textsc{dptr}
)

2:

old\leftarrow\textit{rread}(pt)

3:

if

\textit{is\_mdesc}(old)

then

4:

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}(old)

5:

return
\textit{mread}(pt)

6:

else

7:

return
old

(a)

Helping reading

1:

proc

mread
(
pt:\textsc{dptr}
)

2:

old\leftarrow\textit{rread}(pt)

3:

if

\textit{is\_mdesc}(old)

then

4:

d\leftarrow\textit{rread}(old)

5:

s\leftarrow!({d}.\textit{status})

6:

e\leftarrow
entry for
pt
in
{d}.\textit{entries}

7:

if

s=\textsc{Succ}

then

8:

return
{e}.new

9:

else

10:

return
{e}.exp

11:

else

12:

return
old

(b)

Opportunistic reading

Figure 4
:
Different implementations for
mread
.

Figure
4
shows two possible implementations of the

mread
procedure, realizing two different reading strategies:

helping reading
(Figure

4(a)
), and its optimization

opportunistic reading
(Figure

4(b)
). Both have been introduced
by Harris et al.
[
11
]
; the second one informally.
In the rest of the paper, we assume helping reading, delegating
opportunistic reading to
Appendix
D
.
Nevertheless, we show both implementations here, to emphasize that our
approach applies to both implementations.

The helping reader works as follows. After reading the input pointer
(line
2
), if an MCAS descriptor is found, it
helps the ongoing
mcas
by invoking
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
(line

4
). Then, it starts again
(line
5
).
In contrast, an opportunistic reader optimizes by using the following
strategy. If an MCAS descriptor is found (line

3
), instead of helping,
mread

reads the descriptor’s status (line
5
).
If the status is
Succ
, the descriptor was successfully written
into all input pointers in the ongoing
mcas
(including pointer
pt
,
i.e. the input pointer to
mread
). In this case,

mread
returns the new value for
pt
(as found in the
descriptor) because this is the value
mcas
will write eventually
anyway. If the status is
Fail
,
mread
returns the
expected value for
pt
because
mcas
will leave
pt
unmodified, and
the presence of the descriptor means that
pt
indirectly has the
expected value. If the status is
Undec
,
mread
also
returns the expected value for
pt
because
mread
can pretend
that the read to
pt
occurred before any modification to
pt
, since

mcas
is still attempting to write the descriptor into all the input
pointers. We say that the optimized implementation is an

opportunistic
reader because instead of helping, it takes
advantage of what helpers have registered in the descriptor.
Opportunistic reading is an optimization over helping reading because
it avoids the need to wait for a value to return, as such a wait
increases the response time in the common case when the number of

mread
’s is greater than the number of
mcas

invocations.

3
Introducing and Axiomatizing Visibility

(A_{1})

mcas

(A_{2})
Failing
mcas

if
\exists i\in\overline{u}.\ H({pt}_{i})\neq{exp}_{i}

(a)

{pt}_{i}
(

) in the heap

mutated into
{new}_{i}
, while leaving the rest of

(B_{1})
No in-between

({w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}\wedge w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{{p}})\implies({w^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq{w}\vee{r}\sqsubseteq{w^{\prime}})

(B_{2})
Observed events are writes

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{\_}\implies w\in\mathcal{W}_{{p}}

(B_{3})

(B_{4.1})

(B_{4.2})

\begin{aligned} {\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}&\ x=\textit{mcas}([\ldots,\{{pt}:p,\ {exp}:\_,\ {new}:v\},\ldots])\left<\_\right>\vee{}\\
&\ x=\textit{mwrite}(p,v)\left<\_\right>\vee x=\textit{malloc}(v)\left<p\right>\\
\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\Longleftrightarrow&\ x=\textit{mcas}([\ldots,\{{pt}:p,\ \ldots\},\ldots])\left<true\right>\vee x=\textit{mwrite}(p,\_)\left<\_\right>\vee x=\textit{malloc}(\_)\left<p\right>\\
\end{aligned}

(b)

History-based sequential specification. Relation
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
is
abstract.

:

.

3.1
Sequential History Specifications and Observation Relations

Following Henzinger et al.
[
13
]
, we start the development of
visibility relations by introducing
history-based
specifications
for our data structures.
The idea is that a history-based specification in terms of visibility
relations will be more concrete—and thus more direct to prove—than
merely asserting linearizability of the involved structures.
At the same time, the specification will still be sufficiently
abstract and high-level that: (1) it is largely
shared
by RDCSS
and MCAS, thus providing a unifying explanation of both (and of the
opportunistic reading optimization), and (2) it generically implies
linearizability. We next illustrate the intuition behind history-based
specifications, focusing on the
mcas
procedure.

History-based specifications describe relationships between the data
structure’s procedures in an execution history. They are significantly
different (and more involved) from the perhaps more customary
state-based specifications that describe the actions of a procedure in
terms of input and output state. However, history-based specifications
scale better to the concurrent setting, which is why concurrent
consistency criteria such as linearizability are invariably defined in
terms of execution histories (e.g., see Footnote
1
of Introduction).

In this section we focus on
sequential
histories in order to
introduce the idea of
observation relation
in a simple way,
before generalizing to concurrent histories in
Section
3.2
.
A sequential history is a sequence of the form

[proc(in_{1})\langle out_{1}\rangle,\ \ldots,\ proc(in_{n})\langle out_{n}\rangle]
, where
proc(in_{i})\langle out_{i}\rangle
means that

proc(in_{i})
executed
atomically
and produced output

out_{i}
. We term
event
each element in a sequential history
h
,
and
Ev
denotes the set of all events in
h
.

Figure
5
illustrates the distinction
between sequential state-based and history-based specifications for

mcas
. The state-based specification in
Figure
5(a)
says in axiom
A_{1}
that a
successful
mcas
occurs when all input pointers contain the
expected values; the pointers are then mutated to their new
values. Axiom
A_{2}
says that a failing
mcas
occurs when some
input pointer does not have the expected value, leaving the heap
unchanged.

Figure
5(b)
shows the history sequential
specification for
mcas
. The specification utilizes the

observation relation

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
to
capture a read-write causal dependence between events. In particular,

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}
means that “event
r
reads a value
that event
w
wrote into pointer
p
”.
Under this interpretation,
axioms
B_{1},...,B_{4.2}
state the following
expected properties.
2
2

2

Our paper will make heavy use of several different
relations. To help the reader keep track of them, we shall denote
the relations by symbols that graphically associate to the
relation’s meaning. For example, we use
\lessdot
for the
observation relation, because the symbol graphically resembles an
eye.

Axiom
B_{1}
(No in-between) says that if
r
reads from
w
in
p
,
then no other successful
p
-write can occur between
w
and
r

(otherwise, such a write would overwrite
w
). Relation

\sqsubset
is the
returns-before
relation (with

\sqsubseteq
its reflexive closure), where

{x}\sqsubset{y}
means that
x
terminated before
y
started. Note that
\sqsubset
is a total order on events, as
in a sequential setting the executions of different events cannot
overlap. Set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
collects the successful
p
-writes, e.g.,
writes of the form
\textit{mwrite}(p,\_)
,
malloc
events
returning
p
, and
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
events returning
true
and having
p
in

\overline{u}
.

Axiom
B_{2}
(Observed events are writes) says that any observed event
at
p
must be a successful
p
-write, i.e., if
r
reads from
w
in

p
, then
w
must have actually written into
p
. Axiom
B_{3}

(Dependences occur in the past) says that if a read depends on a
write, then the write executes before the read.

Axioms
B_{4.1}
(Successful
mcas
) and
B_{4.2}
(Failing

mcas
) essentially encode the state-based sequential
specification for
mcas
. For example, axiom
B_{4.1}
directly
says that a successful
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\left<true\right>
event

r
observes—for each of its input entries
\overline{u}
—a successful write
event (
mcas
,
mwrite
or
malloc
, as per axiom
B_{2}
) that
wrote the expected value into the
appropriate pointer. Because the write events are

observed
by
r
, axiom
B_{3}
ensures that they execute before
r
, while
axiom
B_{1}
guarantees the none of them is
overwritten before
r
executes. Thus, diagrammatically,
for each entry
i\in\overline{u}
, the execution
looks as follows, where the write into pointer
{pt}_{i}

persists until the heap
H_{n}
at which
r

executes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\left<true\right>}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{pt}_{i}\mapsto{exp}_{i}\text{ persists}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}}

(
C_{1}
) No in-between

({w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}\wedge w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{{p}})\implies({w^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w}\vee{r}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w^{\prime}})

(
C_{2}
) Observed events are writes

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{\_}\implies w\in\mathcal{W}_{{p}}

(
C_{3}
) No future dependences

x\mathrel{{\prec}^{+}}y\implies{y}\not\sqsubseteq{x}

(
C_{4}
) Return value completion

\exists v.\ \mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}\wedge(x\in T\implies v={x}.\textit{out})

(a)

Concurrent specification. Relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
are
existentially quantified.

General visibility relation

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\bigcup_{p}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}\cup\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

Returns-before relation

{e}\sqsubset{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{e}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{e^{\prime}}.\textit{start}

Set of terminated events

T\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\mid{e}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\}

Closure of terminated events

\overline{T}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\mid\exists t\in T.\ e\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}t\}

\mathcal{Q}_{{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})},{v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}v\in\textsc{Bool}\wedge\begin{cases}\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists w.\ {w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {w}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {{exp}_{i}}&\text{if $v=true$}\\
\exists i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists w.\ \exists v^{\prime}.\ {w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\wedge{}&\text{if $v=false$}\\
\hskip 30.0pt\textsc{writer}\ {w}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {v^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime}\neq{exp}_{i}&\\
\end{cases}

\begin{aligned} \textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\Longleftrightarrow(\exists j\in\overline{u}.\ p={pt}_{j})\wedge\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists w.\ {w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {w}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {{exp}_{i}}\end{aligned}

(b)

Defined notions.

:

. Variables

,

,

,

,

\overline{T}
.

e
,
e^{\prime}

.

\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{W}_{p}
, and

.

). The full list of axioms is shown in
Figure
11
of Appendix
A
.

3.2
Concurrent Specifications and Separable-Before Relations

Concurrent execution histories do not satisfy the sequential axioms in
Figure
5(b)
for two main reasons. First,
concurrent events can
overlap
in real time. As a consequence,
the axioms
B_{1}
(No in-between) and
B_{3}
(Dependencies occur in the
past) are too restrictive, as they force events to be disjoint, due to
the use of the returns-before relation
\sqsubset
.
Second, events can no longer be treated as atomic; thus event’s start
and end times (if the event is terminated) must be taken into
account. As a consequence, axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
and set

\mathcal{W}_{p}
must be modified to account for the output of an
unfinished event not being available yet. We continue using

Ev
for the set of events in the concurrent history. We denote
by
{e}.\textit{start}
,
{e}.\textit{end}
, the start and end time of event

e
, respectively. We use the standard order relation on natural
numbers
<_{\mathbb{N}}
to compare start and end times.

Figure
6
shows the concurrent
specification that addresses the above issues. Importantly, in
addition to the observation relation, the specification utilizes the

separable-before relation

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
to
capture an ordering dependence between events. In particular,

{x}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{y}
means that “event
x
is separable
before
y
because of some logical gap when using shared pointer

p
”.
We now explain how the concurrent specification of
Figure
6
is obtained from the sequential
one in Figure
5(b)
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
is obtained from
B_{1}
by
replacing
\sqsubset
with
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
. The intuition
is that we want to relax the real-time strong separation imposed by

\sqsubset
into a more permissive separation

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
. The latter captures that a gap can be viewed as
existing between possibly overlapping events, forcing one event to be
ordered before another.
3
3

3

The symbol
\ltimes
twists

\sqsubset
, suggesting that the separable-before relation

\ltimes
relaxes (i.e., is a twist on) returns-before
relation
\sqsubset
.
The
definition of
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
is different for different
implementations (thus, the gap is described by logically different
properties in different implementations), but they all satisfy axiom

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
. We shall see in
Section
3.3
the concrete definition of

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
(i.e., the description of the logical gap) for the

mcas
implementation of Figure
3
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-observed-are-writes}}
is unchanged compared to
B_{2}
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
is obtained from
B_{3}
as follows. In the sequential
specification,
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}
was the only relation encoding
dependences between events, but now we have two relations encoding
dependences,
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}
and
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
. The relation

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{\cup_{p}{(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}\cup\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p})}}
thus collects all the dependences, and we call

\prec
the
general
visibility relation.
4
4

4

The
symbol
\prec
reminds us of an eye without an iris,
suggesting that the general visibility relation is “blinder” than

\lessdot
, since
\prec
also contains

\ltimes
.
We can consider modifying
Axiom
B_{3}
into
{x}\prec{y}\implies{x}\sqsubset{y}
to say that
any dependence
x
of
y
must terminate before
y
starts.
However, such a modification of
B_{3}
is too stringent, as it
does not allow
x
to overlap with
y
. Instead, we relax the
conclusion to say that an event cannot depend on itself or events from
the future, i.e.,
{x}\prec{y}\implies{y}\not\sqsubseteq{x}
. Finally,
we get axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
by replacing
\prec
with its transitive
closure
\mathrel{{\prec}^{+}}
to account for
indirect

dependences of
y
; e.g., in
x_{1}\prec x_{2}\prec y
,
event
x_{1}
is an indirect dependence of
y
.
Hence,
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
reads “any direct
or indirect dependence does not execute in the future, and events do
not depend on themselves”.

To understand Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
, we need to consider the set
T
of all

terminated
events and its closure under the general visibility
relation

\overline{T}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\in\textsc{Ev}\mid\exists t\in T.\ e\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}t\}
. As usual,

\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}
is the reflexive-transitive closure of

\prec
.
The idea here is that
\overline{T}
contains all the events that have
contributed to producing values in the execution history, either by
being terminated (and thus directly returning a value), or by being an
event on which some terminated event depends. The dependence may be
indirect, and the depended event may be unterminated. For example, a
terminated
mcas

r
may observe an unterminated
mcas

w
,
i.e.,
{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}{r}
as follows:
w
mutates
p
but is preempted
just before termination, thus allowing
r
to read. At any rate,

\overline{T}
contains all the events required to “explain” the
values obtained in the concurrent execution, and thus establish that
the linearized execution is
sequentially sound
, as per
Footnote
1
. In our example with
r
and

w
, we must consider
w
if we want to explain the return value of

r
, which is why
w
is included in
\overline{T}
.

Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
first coalesces axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
into the
postcondition predicate
\mathcal{Q}
, as shown in
Figure
6(b)
. Then

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
requires that every event
x\in\overline{T}
satisfies

\mathcal{Q}
. In case
x
is a terminated
mcas
event, it is
easy to see that this requirement, along with the requirement that
v

equals the output of
x
, directly corresponds to the sequential
axioms
B_{4.1}
and
B_{4.2}
. In case
x
is an unterminated

mcas
event, the axiom posits that some value
v
can be found to
make
x
appear as if it has terminated in a manner coherent with the
other events. The latter “completion” of
x
with
v
is a common
pattern that we inherit from the work on linearizability.

We further emphasize that in the concurrent setting we also need to
redefine the set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
of successful writes into pointer
p
.
It does not suffice to consider an
mcas
as a successful write if
it returns
true
; we need a criterion when an unfinished
mcas

is a successful write as well. Thus, we define in
Figure
6(b)
that an

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
is a successful
p
-write if
p
is a pointer
in
\overline{u}
, and
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
observes events writing
the expected values for each entry in
\overline{u}
.

\mathcal{Q}_{{\textit{rdcss}(d)},{v}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{aligned} \exists w_{2}.\,{w_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\textit{rdcss}(d)}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {w_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v}\wedge{}\\
(v={d}.exp_{2}\implies\exists w_{1}.\,{w_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{\textit{rdcss}(d)})\end{aligned}

\textit{rdcss}(d)\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{aligned} p={d}.pt_{2}\wedge\exists w_{1},w_{2}.\ {w_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{\textit{rdcss}(d)}\wedge{w_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\textit{rdcss}(d)}\wedge{}\\
\textsc{writer}\ {w_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {{d}.exp_{1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {w_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.exp_{2}}\end{aligned}

Figure 7
:
Together with axioms in
Figure
6(a)
, this is a (fragment of) concurrent specification for
rdcss
.
Full definitions of
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{W}_{p}
, and
writer
for the RDCSS module are in Appendix
A.2
.

Section
4
will show that the axioms in
Figure
6(a)
, henceforth called

visibility axioms
, suffice to prove the existence of a
sequentially sound total order. Thus, proving linearizability for an
MCAS implementation reduces to finding
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}

and
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
that satisfy the visibility axioms.

One can repeat the steps for
rdcss
, and obtain again the axioms in
Figure
6(a)
, but with
\mathcal{W}_{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}
defined
as in Figure
7
.
The postcondition for
\textit{rdcss}(d)
states that
\textit{rdcss}(d)
must observe
an event that wrote
\textit{rdcss}(d)
’s output
v
into
{d}.pt_{2}
,
and in case
v
is the expected value,
\textit{rdcss}(d)
must have read
pointer
{d}.pt_{1}
also. An
\textit{rdcss}(d)
successfully writes
if it observes two events that wrote the expected values into
pointers
{d}.pt_{1}
and
{d}.pt_{2}
.

Figure 8
:
An execution of
mcas
events
A
and
B
. Each event
encircles the spans belonging to it (i.e., helping it). The
intersection of spans for each event are shown as gray
columns. Spans
a
and
b
, both accessing pointer
p
, are named
for later reference.

3.3
Visibility Relations for MCAS

3.3.1
Definitions of Visibility Relations

We now proceed to explain how relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p}
and

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
are defined for the implementation of
mcas
in Figure

3
.
The key idea is to analyze the behavior of the descriptors during the
execution. Figure
8
shows the execution of two
overlapping invocations of
mcas
, which we refer to as events
A

and
B
, respectively.
5
5

5

We focus on the successful

mcas
case. Section
5
will
explain the failing
mcas
case.
During the execution of

mcas
, threads write the descriptor into a pointer
(line
31
in Figure
3
), and then
remove it (line
18
). We term
span
the
time interval between writing the descriptor and removing it. We also
say that the removed descriptor is

resolved
. Figure
8
depicts spans as
bounded line segments.

There are two key invariants of
mcas
that give rise to
its visibility relations.
The first key
invariant is that all the spans of an event
must overlap
(we
call this the
bunching invariant
). Concretely for
mcas

events, any descriptor write must occur before any descriptor
removal. This is so because of the following two properties related to
how control flows through
mcas
. First, threads can reach
line
18
(descriptor removal) only if some
thread changed the descriptor status at
line
14
.
6
6

6

Recall that all descriptors
are
Undec
initially, and can evolve to
Succ
or

Fail
only.
This holds because
line
18
(descriptor removal) is in the

\mathit{false}
branch of the conditional at
line
12
that checks if the descriptor
status is
Undec
. Therefore, if no thread has changed the
descriptor status at line
14
, threads starting
the
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}
procedure will keep entering the
\mathsf{true}

branch at line
12
. The second property
is that any descriptor write at line
31
must
occur before some thread changes the descriptor status at
line
14
. This holds because the
rdcss
at
line
31
(descriptor write attempt) fails if
the descriptor status is no longer
Undec
. These two properties
imply that any descriptor write must occur before the status change,
and any descriptor removal must occur after the status change, i.e.,
any span starts before any span finishes in the event; hence, span
intersection is non-empty. Figure
8
depicts the
spans’ intersection for each event as gray columns. Since the presence
of a descriptor signals that a pointer indirectly has the expected
value, inside the gray column all the event’s pointers simultaneously
have the expected values. Therefore, the event can be considered as
abstractly occurring (i.e., having its LP) anywhere inside the gray
column.

The second key invariant is that spans writing into
the same
pointer
(e.g., spans
a
and
b
in
Figure
8
)
must be disjoint
(we call this
the
disjointness invariant
).
This is so because
mcas
uses helping; thus an event writes into
a pointer only if there is no descriptor currently present. More
specifically, the
rdcss
at line
31
in
Figure
3
writes the descriptor only if the expected value
is present directly (i.e., not via a descriptor). Since spans start
by writing a descriptor, no span can start while there is a descriptor present, or
equivalently, when another span is already active in the pointer. The span
can start only after the currently present descriptor is removed.

These two invariants imply, as also apparent from
Figure
8
, that gray columns of
different events
A
and
B
accessing a common pointer must be
disjoint. Thus, we can
separate
event
A
before event
B
in
time, because
A
’s gray column
executes before
B
’s. In the figure, we can describe the separation
(i.e., the gap between the gray columns) by saying that
there is a span in
A
(namely
a
) and a span in
B
(namely
b
),
both accessing a common pointer, and
a
completes before
b
starts.
Spans
a
and
b
, being disjoint, induce a gap in the
gray columns.

We define the separable-before relation
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
so that
it directly formalizes the above description of the separation between
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle{A}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{B}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\exists x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A),y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B).\ {x}\sqsubset^{S}{y}

(1)

In the definition,
x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A)
means that
x
is a span
accessing pointer
p
in event
A
(similarly for

y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B)
), and
{x}\sqsubset^{S}{y}
means that span
x

terminates before
y
starts.
Relation
\sqsubset^{S}
is a partial order on spans that
totally orders spans accessing the same pointer due to span
disjointness.
\sqsubseteq^{S}
denotes its reflexive closure.

We now focus on the observation relation

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
. We previously informally explained
that
{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}
intuitively captures that event

B
reads a value written by the
p
-write
A
, with no other
intervening writes between.
To illustrate how this intuition can be expressed using spans, consider the
span
b
in Figure
8
.
When this span starts, it reads the input pointer (this is so because the
rdcss
at
line
31
in Figure
3
obtains the
pointer’s value, writes the descriptor and returns the read
value). This obtained value must have been written by the most recent
span that successfully wrote into the pointer, which, in
Figure
8
is span
a
.

To formally capture the described situation, we say that event
A
is

observed
by
B
at pointer
p
if there are spans
x
and
y
in

A
and
B
, respectively, such that
x
is the most recent span that terminated before

y
started, and that successfully wrote into
p
,

\displaystyle{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\exists x\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(A),y\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(B).\ x=\max_{\sqsubseteq^{S}}\{z\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}\mid{z}\sqsubset^{S}{y}\}

(2)

In the definition, the maximum is taken under

\sqsubseteq^{S}
, and set
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
collects the
spans that
successfully
write into
p
. We differentiate
successful writes because some spans do not carry out changes. For
example, line
18
in Figure

3
produces a span that writes the new value only if the
descriptor’s status was set to
Succ
, but produces a span that
undoes changes (as if the descriptor was not written) if the status
was set to
Fail
.
Notice that it makes sense
to take the maximum under
\sqsubseteq^{S}

because spans accessing the same pointer are disjoint, hence,
linearly ordered under
\sqsubseteq^{S}
.

3.3.2
Spans and Visibility Axioms

Section
5
will substantiate more how
definitions (
1
)
and (
2
) satisfy the visibility
axioms of Figure
6(a)
. Here, we
briefly illustrate how the definitions satisfy the no in-between axiom

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
using Figure
8
. In
Figure
8
, assertion

{A}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{B}
means that span
b
reads a value
written by span
a
in pointer
p
. Since
a
is the most recent

p
-writing span before
b
in real-time, and due to span
disjointness, any other
p
-writing span
c
must execute either
completely before
a
or completely after
b
. Therefore, if
c

belongs to some
p
-write
C
, there will be a gap between the gray
columns of either
C
and
A
or
B
and
C
, depending on whether
c

executes before
a
or after
b
.

3.3.3
Alternative Span Definitions

As we just saw, the definitions
(
1
) and
(
2
) satisfy axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
.
Notice, however, that the argument relied solely on the span disjointness
invariant, and not on a specific definition of spans. This is actually
an overarching property that we shall utilize when proving each of the
visibility axioms from Figure
6(a)
.
Neither proof will depend on the particular definition of spans, but
only on high-level abstract span invariants such as bunching,
disjointness and a small number of additional structural ones
that we shall introduce in
Section
5
.

This abstraction affords some freedom to deviate from the operational
definition of spans as the time intervals between writing the descriptor
and resolving it,
so long as the high-level span invariants remain satisfied by the
programs.

To illustrate, bunching and disjointness remain satisfied if the
successful CAS at line
14
is chosen as the
ending point of a span, instead of line
18

that we previously considered. Recall that while explaining the bunching
invariant in Section
3.3.1
,
we stated that any descriptor write must occur before the status change
at line
14
. Therefore, the successful CAS at line
14
is perfectly
fine to end spans, because any span must start before it, i.e., bunching
holds because any span starts before the CAS, which is the ending point for all the spans.
Disjointness still holds because of the same reason as in
Section
3.3.1
: no descriptor can be
written if there is currently a descriptor present. Therefore, if we end the span
prematurely at line
14
, still no span can start until the descriptor
is removed at line
18
much later.

Additionally, single reads that return a
Val
value can be treated as
spans that write a descriptor and instantaneously resolve it (i.e. a “collapsed span”).
For example, the read at line
2
of Figure
4(a)
.
It is essential that we treat as spans only those reads that return
Val
values
to ensure the span disjointness invariant: if we treat reads that
return descriptors as spans, then the read will occur while a descriptor is present,
i.e., inside another span, violating disjointness.

3.3.4
Visibility Relations for RDCSS and Opportunistic Readers

Perhaps surprisingly, definitions
(
1
) and
(
2
) apply without change not
only to
mcas
, but to all events using helping in RDCSS and MCAS
implementations such as
mcas
,
mwrite
,
mread
,

rdcss
,
rread
, etc.

For example,
rdcss
events can be seen as a special case of
Figure
8
, in which each
rdcss
event
executes at most two spans. The first span of
rdcss
is generated
by writing the descriptor at line
11
in Figure

2
, and resolved at either line

24
or line

26
. The second span consists of a single
read of the control pointer at line
22
(thus, it
is a “collapsed” span). Trivially,
rdcss
satisfies the bunching
invariant because the thread that resolves the descriptor at either
line
24
or line

26
, must have also previously read the
control pointer at line
22
while the descriptor
was present. In other words, the collapsed span overlaps the first
span. Also,
rdcss
satisfies span disjointness: an
rdcss

invocation cannot write a new descriptor into a pointer if there is
currently another descriptor present.

On the other hand, opportunistic reading requires definitions
different from (
1
)
and (
2
) (see
Definition
D.5
in
Appendix
D
).
Nevertheless, the concepts of separable-before and observation relations
satisfying the axioms in
Figure
6(a)
, are general
high-level characteristics of all these algorithms.

3.4
Comparing Visibility Relations and Linearization Points

The standard way of proving a concurrent algorithm linearizable is to
exhibit the algorithm’s LPs.
For example, the LPs for
rdcss
from Figure
2
have
been described operationally in previous
work
[
11
,
21
,
15
]
: one LP occurs at
line
11
whenever the
CAS
in
line
11
fails and the value returned by the

CAS
is not a descriptor; another LP occurs at
line
22
, as long as the thread that executes this
line can successfully execute one of the
CAS
s at lines

24
and
26
,

later on
.
While a proof that this is a correct description of the LPs of

rdcss
exists (e.g., in Jung et. al.
[
15
]
), the LP assignment
alone does not really provide much intuition as to the underlying
principles behind
rdcss
, or how these principles are shared with

mcas
.

For comparison, we can recover the LP approach by defining the
visibility relations in terms of LPs. For example, defining the
separable-before relation as,

\displaystyle{x}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{y}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}LP(x)\sqsubset LP(y)

(3)

where
LP(x)
and
LP(y)
are the linearization points of
x
and
y
,
respectively. However,
(
3
) leads to a less
abstract and less modular proof than ours.
Regarding abstraction,
(
3
) does not bring out
that span bunching and disjointness are the two high-level invariants
that
rdcss
and
mcas
share, and that suffice to describe
separation between events for both algorithms.
Regarding modularity,
(
3
) defines a total
order on events
right away
. But notice that the specification
in Figure
6(a)
does not require the
relation
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
to be total, not even over
events sharing pointer
p
; it
only
requires to somehow
separate the events that share
p
. In particular,
(
1
) is not a total order over
events sharing pointer
p
. Instead, the visibility approach divides the
burden of proving linearizability into two stages that are easier to
conquer than directly proving linearizability. In the first stage, we
define the visibility relations and prove the visibility axioms. In the
second stage, the (total) linearization order is constructed out of
the visibility relations. Importantly, the second stage is generic and
independent of the first, as it can be developed out of the visibility
axioms alone, without having the definitions of the visibility
relations in hand.

Where the LP approach very concretely and operationally locates the LP
of each event, the visibility approach is more concerned with
capturing the semantic structure of the algorithms, such as, in the
example of RDCSS and MCAS, recognizing that each event is
characterized by the disjoint bunches of its spans.
In fact, this semantic structure
appears to capture how Harris et al.
[
11
]
themselves thought about these
algorithms, as they reveal in this quote from their Section
4.2:

Each invocation of RDCSS can be considered separately. This
surprising observation follows by examining the memory
accesses. […] Different RDCSS operations acting on the same
location are thereby serialized by the order of their active
periods, so we can consider them individually.

Clearly, “active periods” corresponds to spans, and “serialization
by the order of active periods” corresponds to the separable-before
relation. On the other hand, Harris et al.
[
11
]
do not pursue
axiomatization, which is why no concept analogous to the observation
relation appears for them. Be it as it may, the quote already
contrasts with the (arguably, not very intuitive) description of the
LPs of
rdcss
we gave at the beginning of this section.
That visibility relations can formalize this (shared) intuition behind
algorithms such as RDCSS and MCAS, is the point of our paper.

4
Proving Linearizability out of Visibility Axioms

Having provided a high-level overview, we now proceed with the
technical development of the proofs of linearizability that are

abstract
, i.e., they proceed from the visibility axioms, and
apply to arbitrary implementations of RDCSS and MCAS (the gray box in
Figure
1
). In this section we exhibit the
abstract proof for MCAS. The one for RDCSS is in
Appendix
A.2
. Later on, we shall
instantiate the abstract proofs to conclude that the
concrete

implementations are linearizable. In particular, the helping MCAS
implementation is shown linearizable in
Section
5
, the RDCSS
implementation in Appendix
C.1
, and
the opportunistic MCAS implementation in
Appendix
D
.

Definition 4.1

(Linearizability)
.

We say that a data structure’s implementation
D
is

linearizable
if for any set of events
Ev

generated from an arbitrary execution history in
D
, there is a
binary relation

{\prec}\subseteq{\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}}

(
general visibility relation
) and a linear order

{\leq}\subseteq{\overline{T}\times\overline{T}}
(
linearization
)
7
7

7

\overline{T}
is the closure of the terminated events
T
under
\prec
, as in Figure
6(b)
.
s.t.,

•

\leq
respects the real-time ordering of events,
i.e.,
\sqsubset
restricted to
\overline{T}
is contained in
\leq
.

•

\leq
respects the ordering constraints in
\prec
,
i.e.,
\prec
restricted to
\overline{T}
is
contained in
\leq
.

•

\leq
is
sequentially sound
, i.e., when events are executed in
the order determined by
\leq
, the execution output
o_{i}
of the

i
-th event
x_{i}
matches
{x_{i}}.\textit{out}
if

x_{i}\in T
or it is consistent to define

{x_{i}}.\textit{out}
as
o_{i}
if
x_{i}\notin T
. The
operational semantics of executing events in the order of
\leq
is
data-structure specific.

Our definition of linearizability differs slightly from the usual
formulation
[
14
]
in that it emphasizes working with
visibility relations, as follows. When judging a history
h

linearizable, the standard definition starts by requiring an

arbitrary
completion
\hat{h}
, which closes up some
non-terminated events in
h
. For us, the completion is not arbitrary,
but is generated by the specific visibility relation that we work
with, as captured by the set
\overline{T}
. It is thus easy to see
that our definition implies the standard one. Our definition is
justified, because in practice of proving linearizability, the choice
of the completion is always guided by an implicit notion of dependence
between events; we merely make this notion explicit by means of

\prec
.

4.1
Constructing the linearization order

Given arbitrary
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
, we denote by

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
the specification for MCAS
in Figure
6
.
We first give an overview of the structure of the proof.

The proof is divided into two steps.
The first step constructs a linear order
\leq
containing both

\prec
and
\sqsubset
(the first two requirements in Definition
4.1
) and
satisfying:

\displaystyle{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{y}\implies\neg\exists z\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\cap\overline{T}.\ x<z<y

(4)

i.e., there are no
p
-writes in between a
p
-observation in the
linear order.
The second step consists in proving that
\leq
is sequentially sound
(the third requirement in Definition
4.1
)
by using (
4
), which ensures that
the order of any mutation performed by
x
remains unchanged in
\leq

up to the point where
y
reads. We assume throughout that
the visibility axioms of
V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

hold.

The first step of the proof is independent of the particular definitions for the postcondition predicate

\mathcal{Q}
and the successful writes set
\mathcal{W}_{p}
in
V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
.
Hence, to generalize the first step, we introduce the following definition. Given
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{p},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{p}
, we say that a
specification
, denoted
V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, is a pair
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{W}_{p})
.
8
8

8

This definition is extended in Definition
A.1
of Appendix
A.1

to include allocation events. We elide such events here, as they do not modify the intuition behind the presentation.

A
valid
specification
V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
satisfies the axioms in Figure
6(a)
.

We now focus on the first step, i.e., showing the existence of the
linear order, for a valid specification.
We sketch the construction here, with the full proof in
Appendix
A.1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
and
valid

specification

V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, there is a linear order

\leq
over
\overline{T}
, such that

{({\prec}\cup{\sqsubset})}\subseteq{\leq}
and property
(
4
) holds.

Proof sketch.

Define relation
{\vartriangleleft}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{(\sqsubset\cup\prec)^{+}}
, i.e., the transitive closure of the union of the general visibility
and returns-before relations. Let
\trianglelefteq
be its reflexive closure.
We show that
\trianglelefteq
is a partial order and can be extended into a linear order.

First, axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
ensures that

\trianglelefteq
is a partial order over
\overline{T}
(Lemma

A.5
in Appendix

A.1
). Intuitively,

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-future-dependence}}
states that events do not look
for dependencies into their real-time future, so that cycles will not
be inserted when
\prec
is combined with

\sqsubset
, ensuring irreflexivity of
\vartriangleleft
.

Second, given an
arbitrary
partial order extension
\leq
of

\trianglelefteq
(over domain
\overline{T}
), axiom

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
ensures property
(
4
). Indeed, if there is such write
z
, then
axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
says that
z
is separable
before
x
or after
y
. But
\leq
contains
\trianglelefteq

(hence the separable-before relation as well), which means that
z

must also occur in
\leq
before
x
or after
y
(Contradiction).

Third, since any partial order can be extended to a linear
order,
9
9

9

See topological sorting
algorithms
[
16
]
.
choose some linear order
\leq

extending
\trianglelefteq
. Notice that
\leq
will satisfy
(
4
) because it is a partial order extension
of
\trianglelefteq
. Also,
\leq
contains

{\prec}
and
{\sqsubset}
, since
\leq
contains

\trianglelefteq
by construction.
∎

Now that we have a linear order
\leq
that respects both

\prec
and
\sqsubset
,
for the second step in the linearizability proof
we show that
\leq
is sequentially sound.

We need to show that executing the events in the order of
\leq

produces outputs that match the outputs registered in each event. To
define the notion of execution of events in MCAS, we introduce a
state-based sequential operational semantics. States for MCAS are
memory heaps. Let us denote by

H\xrightarrow{proc(in)\ \langle out\rangle}H^{\prime}
the statement that
procedure
proc
with input
in
executes atomically on heap
H
,
produces output
out
and modifies the heap into
H^{\prime}
. We can then
define the effect of the
mcas
procedure on a heap by the
following operational semantics (we will focus only on
mcas
, for
the rest of procedures in MCAS, see
Definition
A.13
in
Appendix
A.3
),

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle false\rangle}H
, if for some
j\in\overline{u}
,

H({pt}_{j})\neq{exp}_{j}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle true\rangle}{H}[{{{pt}_{j}}\mapsto{{new}_{j}}}]_{j\in\overline{u}}
, if for every
j\in\overline{u}
,
H({pt}_{j})={exp}_{j}
.

The first case corresponds to a failing execution in which some input
pointer does not have the expected value, while the second case
corresponds to a successful execution in which all input pointers have
their expected values and the pointers mutate into their new
values.

Executing
\leq
then translates to proving the existence of a path in
the operational semantics such that the
i
-th event
x_{i}
in
\leq

matches the
i
-th procedure in the path, and if

x_{i}\in T
, then
{x_{i}}.\textit{out}
matches the
i
-th
output in the path. Notice that the existence of the path implicitly
assigns an output to those events in

\overline{T}\setminus T
.

The hypothesis of validity of the specification
V^{\textsc{M}}

for MCAS, will ensure the existence of such a path, as the following
lemma shows (the full proof is in
Appendix
A.3
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Given relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
such that

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is valid, the linear
order
\leq
of Lemma
4.1
is
sequentially sound.

Proof sketch.

We prove by induction on
n
the following claim, so that sequential
soundness follows by applying the claim with

n=\lvert\overline{T}\rvert
.

Claim
.

For any

1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}\lvert\overline{T}\rvert
, there is a path of length
n
starting from the empty heap
that matches
\leq
from the
1
-th event to the
n
-th event.

We focus on the inductive case, as the base case
n=1
must have an
alloc as first event in
\leq

(see Lemma
A.17
in
Appendix
A.3

for details). The inductive hypothesis provides a path
of length
n
that matches
\leq
up to the
n
-th event,

\displaystyle H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \left<out_{1}\right>}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \left<out_{2}\right>}H_{2}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \left<out_{n}\right>}H_{n}

(5)

We need to show that we can extend the path with a
(n+1)-st
step
that matches the
(n+1)-st
event in
\leq
. We case analyze the

(n+1)-st
event. Let us focus on the
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
case (see Lemma

A.17
in
Appendix
A.3
for the other cases).

From Axiom
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
and the definition of

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, we have that event

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
must satisfy for some

v\in\textsc{Bool}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {{exp}_{i}}&\text{ if }v=true\\
\exists i\in\overline{u},v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Val}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {v^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime}\neq{{exp}_{i}}&\text{ if }v=false\end{cases}

Focusing on the
v=true
case, event
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
must be observing
at every input pointer
{pt}_{i}
an event
z
that wrote the
expected value
{exp}_{i}
into
{pt}_{i}
. Notice that

z
must be a write thanks to Axiom

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-observed-are-writes}}
. Since path
(
5
) matches
\leq
and
z
is an event
occurring before
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
(i.e.,

{z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}

implies
z<\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
),
z
must be one of the
proc_{k}
’s in the
path, which means that
H_{k}({pt}_{i})={exp}_{i}
. In
addition, property (
4
) ensures that there
is no other write to pointer
{pt}_{i}
between
z
and

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
, which means that there is no
{pt}_{i}
-write
in path (
5
) from
proc_{k}
up to

proc_{n}
, having as consequence that

H_{n}({pt}_{i})={exp}_{i}
(see Lemma

A.15
in Appendix

A.3
). Since this happens with every input
pointer, heap
H_{n}
contains the expected values in the input
pointers, so that we can augment path
(
5
) with the step

H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle true\rangle}{H_{n}}[{{{pt}_{i}}\mapsto{{new}_{i}}}]_{i\in\overline{u}}
. In case
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\in T
, Axiom

C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-return-completion}}
also ensures that

true=v={\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}.\textit{out}
, so that the new step matches

\leq
. The case
v=false
is similar.
∎

Together, Lemmas
4.1

and
4.2
imply the following
theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

Given an implementation of MCAS, suppose that for any set of
events
Ev
generated from an arbitrary execution history in
the implementation, there are relations

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
such that

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}})
is valid. Then, the
implementation is linearizable.

Proof.

Let
Ev
be a set of events generated from an arbitrary
execution history in the implementation.
From the hypothesis,
V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}})
is valid
for some
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
and

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma

4.1
. Then, we take
\prec
and
\leq
to be the
relations required by Definition
4.1
.
By construction,
\leq
respects both
\prec
and

\sqsubset
. Also,

\leq
is sequentially sound by Lemma

4.2
.
∎

5
Axiomatizing Spans and Proving Visibility

This section fills the remaining gap in our proofs of linearizability
and concludes the technical development. Again, we illustrate with the
helping MCAS implementation, relegating RDCSS and opportunistic MCAS
to Appendices
C.1

and
D
, respectively.

To prove that the implementation in Figure
3
is
linearizable,
Theorem
4.1
requires
exhibiting two relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
that make the MCAS specification

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
valid.
We will show that the separable-before
(
1
) and observation
(
2
) relations from Section

3.3
suffice. In this section,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
and
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}

refer to (
1
) and
(
2
), respectively.
As apparent,
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
and

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
are defined in terms of spans. While
Section
3.3
describes spans informally,
here we make the definitions formal and abstract, via a notion of

span structure
.

We shall postulate a number of axioms that a span structure must
satisfy. Our proof obligations for validity of

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
then split into two parts. First, we
show that if the span structure we define for MCAS satisfies the
axioms, then

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is valid, and thus, by
Theorem
4.1
, MCAS is
linearizable. Second, we show that the span structure for MCAS
satisfies the axioms. We remark that the first part of the proof is
actually more general, as it also applies to RDCSS; indeed, it only
uses the span axioms and definitions
(
1
) and
(
2
), which are shared with RDCSS
(see Appendix
B
).

5.1
Intuition

(a)

Successful execution.

(b)

Failing execution.

Figure 9
:
Spans of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
. Full spans represent
successful help attempts, and are shown as line
segments. Collapsed spans represent failed help attempts,
and are represented as dots. Encircled spans are the ones
chosen to formally denote the execution of the

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
event. All spans write into the same
descriptor. All full spans share the same ending rep
event—the successful CAS at line
14

that changes the descriptor status—represented as a
vertical line on the right. The span with
\checkmark

indicates that the thread that started the span,
successfully executed the CAS at
line
14
.

As mentioned in
Section
3.3.3
, we can
use many different definitions of spans, as long as they satisfy the
span invariants (i.e., span axioms, to be presented in
Section
5.2
). In particular, we shall use
two different kinds of spans: full and collapsed spans, illustrated in
Figure
9
as line segments and dots,
respectively.
A
full span
starts when the successful
rdcss
at
line
31
in Figure
3
writes the
descriptor, and finishes when the descriptor status is updated at
line
14
, as explained in
Section
3.3.3
. A

collapsed span
is any failing
rdcss
at
line
31
that returns the non-expected
Val
value; thus,
a collapsed span corresponds to a failed help attempt.
Figure
9
shows how full and collapsed
spans interact in two kinds of executions of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.

Successful execution
of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})

(Figure
9(a)
) occurs when a descriptor

d
is created at line
24
containing the entries in

\overline{u}
, each full span writes the descriptor
d
into each entry
pointer of
\overline{u}
, and the thread that executed the last full
span sets the descriptor status to
Succ
(indicated in the
figure with a
\checkmark
).

While the actual successful execution of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
may
witness both kinds of spans (as evident in
Figure
9(a)
), it is only the full spans
writing
d
that explain the successful outcome. Indeed, a collapsed
span may arise if the considered pointer did not contain the expected
value when help was attempted. However, such a failed help attempt
must be followed by a full span at the same pointer, or else the
execution of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
would not be successful. Such a
full span overrides any prior collapsed span at the same pointer, and,
along with other full spans, ultimately explains the overall outcome
of the
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.

This is why we use the set of full spans as the semantic abstraction
(i.e.,
denotation
) of the successful execution of

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
, and represent this in
Figure
9(a)
by encircling the full spans
only.
It is this denotation that our span axioms apply
to. For example, we introduced the bunching invariant in
Section
3.3.1
to hold
of a number of spans if they share a common subinterval. As evident
from Figure
9(a)
, bunching holds of the
encircled spans. However, it would fail if, for example, the collapsed
span (dot) on
p_{2}
were to be encircled as well, since this dot does
not overlap with the segment on
p_{n}
.

Failing execution
of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})

(Figure
9(b)
) occurs when a descriptor
d

is created at line
24
containing the entries

\overline{u}
, and:

•

there is an entry
j\in\overline{u}
such that some
collapsed span failed to write descriptor
d
into the
j
-th pointer,

•

there are full spans that write descriptor
d
into each pointer of
entries
i<j
, and

•

the thread that executed the collapsed span for
the
j
-th entry managed to set the descriptor status to
Fail

using the CAS in line
14
(the collapsed span
for the
j
-th entry is indicated in the figure with a
\checkmark
).

As encircled in Figure
9(b)
, the full spans
writing
d
for entries
i<j
, along with the
\checkmark
-ed
collapsed span, explain the failed outcome of
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
,
and thus we use this set of spans as the denotation for the failed

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
event.

The collapsed spans for entries
i<j
, the full spans following the

\checkmark
-ed collapsed span, and the full and collapsed spans for
entries
i>j
, do not influence the
Fail
outcome of

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
, because the
\checkmark
-ed collapsed span is
the one responsible for setting the status to
Fail
. In other
words, the thread executing the
\checkmark
-ed span ultimately
confirms the failure by setting the descriptor status to
Fail
at
the CAS.

As in the successful case, we apply our span axioms to
the denotation of a failing
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
. For example,
while bunching holds for the encircled spans in
Figure
9(b)
, it would fail if we
additionally encircled the full span on
p_{j}
.

5.2
Axiomatizing Spans

We now formalize spans via the notion of span
structure, so that invariants (now called span axioms), like bunching
and disjointness, are stated independently of the span definitions
(e.g., as in
Section
3.3.2
, where the
argument for
C_{\ref{vis-ax::cc-no-in-between}}
depended solely on
the disjointness invariant, and not on the span definition). We first
need a helper notion of rep events.

Rep events.

Rep events
are generated by the invocation of a code line
inside a procedure. For example, invoking line

14
in Figure
3
produces a rep
event.
We do not assume that rep events are
atomic, because events in
Ev
may become rep events from the point
of view of another implementation; for example, the exportable procedures in
the RDCSS implementation are used as primitive procedures in MCAS.
If
x
is a rep event,
{x}.\textit{start}
,
{x}.\textit{end}
, and
{x}.\textit{out}

denotes its start time, ending time, and output, respectively.
Since rep events are not atomic, we assume that we are given a
linearization
\leq^{R}
on rep events, and use
[\leq^{R}]

to denote the domain of this relation. The set of all rep events in an
execution history is denoted as
Rep
. The distinction between
events (
Ev
) and rep events is standard in the theory of
linearizability
[
14
]
.

Span Structure.

A
span structure

(\mathcal{S}_{p},\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p},\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket)
consists
of:

•

For every pointer
p
, a set
\mathcal{S}_{p}\subseteq\textsc{Rep}\times\textsc{Rep}
, called the
spans
accessing
p
.

•

For every pointer
p
, a set
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{p}
, called the
successful write spans
into
p
.

•

A function
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket:\textsc{Ev}\rightarrow(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{p}\mathcal{S}_{p})\times(\textsc{Val}\cup\{\bot\}))
,
called
event denotation
, written
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
for event
x
. Here,
\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{p}\mathcal{S}_{p})
denotes the power set of
\bigcup_{p}\mathcal{S}_{p}
.

Each span in
\mathcal{S}_{p}
is a pair of rep events, encoding the end
points of the span. We denote as
f
(“first”)
and
l
(“last”) the standard pair projection
functions. A collapsed span has coinciding end-points
(a,a)
, and is
abbreviated as
(a)
.

Set
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
collects spans that mutate state, for example,
those full spans in Figure
9(a)
that set
their descriptor status to
Succ
.

The denotation
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
for event
x\in\textsc{Ev}

returns a pair, whose first component is the set of spans belonging to

x
, and whose second component is the output associated
with the denotation (
\bot
if no output is defined). Intuitively,
the denotation models the closed path encircling the spans in
Figure
9
, together with its
associated output.

We define
\mathcal{S}(x)\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\pi_{1}({\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket})
to
be the set of spans in the denotation of
x
;

\mathcal{S}_{p}(x)\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\mathcal{S}(x)\cap\mathcal{S}_{p}
to be the set of

p
-accessing spans in the denotation of
x
.
10
10

10

This is the
notation we already used in Section

3.3
and in definitions
(
1
) and
(
2
).
Also, we define

o({x})\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\pi_{2}({\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket})

to be the denotation output of event
x
.
11
11

11

It is essential to not
confuse
o({x})
with the event’s output

{x}.\textit{out}
. An event
x
may have
{x}.\textit{out}
undefined
because
x
has not terminated, but still have

o({x})
defined because
x
executed the
corresponding spans and is ready to terminate. For terminated
events, the two values coincide.

Span returns-before relation.

Relation

{b}\sqsubset^{S}{c}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{l({b})}<^{R}{f({c})}

states that span
b
terminates before
c
starts in the linearization
of rep events.
{b}\sqsubset^{S}{c}
can be interpreted as “
b

resolves its descriptor before
c
writes its own”.

Implementation by a span structure.

Given arbitrary relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
, we say that

V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
(which is a pair

(\mathcal{W}_{p},\mathcal{Q})
) is
implemented by span
structure

(\mathcal{S}_{p},\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p},\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket)
if all the
span axioms in Figure
10
are
satisfied. We also say that

V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is
implemented by spans
if
there exists some span structure that implements it.
The idea is that the span axioms imply the validity of

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, as per the following theorem, which
thus, together with
Theorem
4.1
,
reduces linearizability to proving the span axioms.

Theorem 5.1
.

If

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is implemented by spans, then

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is valid.
12
12

12

This is
theorem
B.1

stated in a more general form in
Appendix
B
, here
instantiated with
\mu=\textsc{M}
.

Span returns-before relation

{b}\sqsubset^{S}{c}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{l({b})}<^{R}{f({c})}

Set of spans in denotation

\mathcal{S}(x)\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\pi_{1}({\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket})

Denotation output

o({x})\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\pi_{2}({\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket})

(a)

Defined notions for span axioms.

(
S_{1}
) Disjointness

(
S_{2}
) Bunching

b,c\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\implies({{b}\sqsubset^{S}{c}}\vee{{c}\sqsubset^{S}{b}}\vee{b=c})

b,c\in\mathcal{S}(x)\implies{f({b})}\leq^{R}{l({c})}

(
S_{3}
) Adequacy

x\in T\implies o({x})={x}.\textit{out}\wedge\mathcal{S}(x)\neq\emptyset

(b)

Key Axioms

(
S_{4}
)

{f({b})}\leq^{R}{l({b})}

(
S_{5}
)

\mathcal{S}(x)\cap\mathcal{S}(y)\neq\emptyset\implies x=y

(
S_{6}
)

b\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}\implies\exists x.\ b\in\mathcal{S}(x)

(
S_{7}
)

\mathcal{S}(x)\neq\emptyset\implies o({x})\neq\bot\wedge\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{o({x})}}

(
S_{8}
)

\mathcal{S}(x)\neq\emptyset\implies(x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{S}(x)\cap\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}\neq\emptyset)

(
S_{9}
)

(i)
b\in\mathcal{S}(x)\implies{x}.\textit{start}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{f({b})}.\textit{start}

(ii)
(b\in\mathcal{S}(x)\wedge x\in T)\implies{}

\exists i\in[\leq^{R}].\ {i}.\textit{end}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{end}\wedge{l({b})}\leq^{R}{i}

(c)

Structural Axioms

Figure 10
:
Span axioms for span structure
(\mathcal{S}_{p},\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p},\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket)
implementing
V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})=(\mathcal{W}_{p},\mathcal{Q})
. Variables
x
,
y
range over
Ev
. Variables
b
,
c
over
\bigcup_{p}\mathcal{S}_{p}
. Variable
p
over
pts
. The list omits three axioms involving allocs, which are shown in Figure
12
of Appendix
B
.

We now explain the span axioms. The first two axioms formalize the
