disjointness and bunching invariants from
Section
3.3
. The remaining axioms relate
the code to the span structure and formalize book-keeping invariants
required for the proofs.

Key Axioms.

Disjointness Axiom

S_{\ref{span::descriptors-do-not-interfere}}
states that
spans accessing the same pointer
p
do not overlap, i.e.,
any descriptor in
p
must be resolved before another one is written
in
p
.

Bunching Axiom
S_{\ref{span::all-descriptors-are-written-before-any-resolution}}

states that any two spans executed by the same event must
overlap. In other words, no descriptor can be resolved unless all
descriptors pertinent to the event have been written first (i.e.,

f({b})\leq^{R}l({c})
, pairwise, for all spans

b
,
c
in the event’s denotation).

Adequacy Axiom
S_{\ref{span::finished-operations-have-a-run}}

states that for terminated events, the denotation output coincides with
the event’s output, and the event contains at least one span. This
axiom is proved by showing that executing the actual code produces the
denotation. For example, proving adequacy for an
mcas
event amounts
to showing that the code does produce the spans encircled in Figures

9(a)
and
9(b)
,
as shown in Theorem
5.2
of
Section
5.3
.

Structural Axioms.

Axiom

S_{\ref{span::descriptor-write-precedes-resolution}}

justifies calling functions
f
,

l
the first and last rep events in a span. The
axiom states that writing a descriptor precedes its resolution.

Axiom
S_{\ref{span::runs-are-injective}}

states that denotations of different events do not share spans.

Axiom
S_{\ref{span::writer-blocks-belong-to-runs}}
states
that successful writer spans must belong to the denotation of some event
(i.e. mutations do not occur “out of thin air”). Although it is
possible to strengthen the axiom to
all
spans, this
is not required for correctness since non-writer spans can
spuriously occur in the execution history as they do not have an effect
that could be discovered by other spans.

Axiom
S_{\ref{span::postcondition-predicate-holds}}
considers
an event
x
, which has completed its execution, though it need not
have terminated yet. We identify the completion of the execution with
the set of spans in
x
’s denotation being nonempty
(i.e.
\mathcal{S}(x)\neq\emptyset
). The axiom then says that
x

produced a denotation output and
x
’s postcondition predicate must hold at

x
’s denotation output, as if
x
has terminated.

Axiom
S_{\ref{span::writers-have-writer-blocks}}
states that
successful
p
-write events must contain a successful
p
-writer span in their denotation.

Axiom

S_{\ref{span::blocks-contained-in-abstract-time-interval}}

describes the relation between an event’s duration and the spans in
its denotation. The first part of Axiom

S_{\ref{span::blocks-contained-in-abstract-time-interval}}
says that
spans in a denotation start (in real time) after the event starts. The
second part of Axiom

S_{\ref{span::blocks-contained-in-abstract-time-interval}}
says that
spans in a denotation finish (according to the rep linearization) before
some rep event
i
that witnesses the event’s end in real-time. For
the second part, we cannot require the stronger property

{l({b})}.\textit{end}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{end}
because spans
may be ended by threads different from the one invoking
x
, which
means that
l({b})
could still be executing by the time
x

finishes.
13
13

13

Recall that rep events are not required to be atomic.

5.3
Proving the Span Axioms for MCAS

By Theorem
5.1
, the problem of
proving linearizability of MCAS reduces to defining a span
structure
S^{\textsc{M}}
that implements the MCAS specification

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
.
Here we define
S^{\textsc{M}}
by using the code for
mcas
in
Figure
3
. Appendix
C.2

gives the full definition of
S^{\textsc{M}}
using the full code in
Figure
14
, which contains procedures elided from
Figure
3
. Then, we show that

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is implemented by span structure

S^{\textsc{M}}
, i.e, that
mcas
satisfies the span axioms.

Intuitively, structure
S^{\textsc{M}}
encodes the ideas presented in
Section
5.1
.
Set
\mathcal{S}_{p}
contains all full and collapsed spans
accessing pointer
p
, defined as follows,

•

A
full
span at pointer
p
is a tuple
(a,b)
of rep events

a
,
b
, where
a
is the successful
rdcss
at
line
31
writing some descriptor
d
in
p
,
and
b
is the successful CAS at line
14

updating
d
’s status to either
Succ
or
Fail
.

•

A
collapsed
span at pointer
p
is
(c)
, where rep event
c

is an
rdcss
at line
31
that fails to
write a descriptor into
p
(i.e.,
c
returns a
Val
value
different from the expected value).

Set
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
contains all spans
(a,b)\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
where

b
updates the status to

Succ
. The denotation
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
for
x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
is defined
by cases, encoding the two kinds of
executions discussed in Section
5.1
,

•

(Successful execution)

\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}(\{(a_{i},b)\in\mathcal{S}_{{pt}_{i}}\mid i\in\overline{u}\},\textit{true})
,
where each
a_{i}
wrote in
{pt}_{i}
the same descriptor
d
(created at line
24
) containing the entries
\overline{u}
,
and
b
is the unique CAS at line
14
that successfully updated
d
’s status to
Succ
.

•

(Failing execution)

\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}(\{(a_{i},b)\in\mathcal{S}_{{pt}_{i}}\mid i<j\}\cup\{(c)\},\textit{false})
,
where each
a_{i}
wrote in
{pt}_{i}
the same descriptor
d
(created at line
24
) containing the entries
\overline{u}
;

b
is the unique CAS at line
14
that successfully updated
d
’s status to
Fail
;
b
and
c

are executed by some thread
T
such that

c
is the failed
rdcss
at line
31
that attempted to write
d
and is the most recent such
rdcss
preceding
b
in the control
flow of
T
; and
j
is the entry in which
c
failed to write
d
(i.e.,
c
failed to write in pointer
{pt}_{j}
).

•

\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}(\emptyset,\bot)
, if the history does not satisfy the previous two cases.

In both non-empty cases of the definition of
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
, full spans
are uniquely determined, because the descriptor
d
is unique to the

mcas
invocation. Moreover, once
b
changes
d
’s status,
d
cannot
be written nor its status be changed, since both the
rdcss
and CAS at
lines
31
,
14
expect the
Undec

status. Hence, for each
i
,
\mathcal{S}_{{pt}_{i}}
contains at
most one full span writing
d
. Additionally, in the failing
execution,
(c)
is uniquely determined because it is executed by the same
thread
T
that executed
b
. More specifically, when
T
executes
c
at line
31

(descriptor write attempt), immediately after
c
fails,
T
exits the
writeall
procedure at
line
13
and executes
b
(descriptor status
change), meaning that
c
is the instruction immediately preceding
b
in the control
flow of
T
.

The empty case of the definition of
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
is when the denotation
is undefined. It is the role of the adequacy
axiom
S_{\ref{span::finished-operations-have-a-run}}
to ensure that
this case does not arise for terminated events, and therefore that the denotation
correctly captures the spans that are executed by the code of a terminated

mcas
.

With this definition for
S^{\textsc{M}}
, we have the following
theorem, which together with theorems

4.1
and

5.1
implies that
mcas
is
linearizable.

Theorem 5.2
.

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

is implemented by
span structure
S^{\textsc{M}}
.

Proof sketch.

We consider only the case of the
mcas
procedure in
Figure
3
, focusing on the proof of the adequacy axiom,
in the case when
mcas
returns
\mathit{true}
. The proof of the
\mathit{false}
case and the other span axioms
are found in their respective lemmas in
Appendix
C.2
, which uses the full
code in Figure
14
.

Since
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
returned
true
, line
15
in Figure
3
must have read the status pointer of descriptor
d

to be
Succ
(where
d
was created at line
24
).
This implies that some thread
T^{\prime}
(which could be
T
as well)
must have set
d
’s status pointer to
Succ
at line
14
, and
T^{\prime}
must
have finished the execution of
writeall
with a
Succ
at line
13
.
Since
writeall
is a recursive method which only recurses when it needs to try again, and we know that
T^{\prime}

finished executing
writeall
,
T^{\prime}
must have reached
an invocation of
writeall
that did not recurse, i.e., an
invocation of
writeall
where
lines
34
-
35
were not executed. So, we
can assume we are on such execution.

Since
writeall
returned
Succ
,
thread
T^{\prime}
must have tried all the entries inside the loop.
For each entry
i\in\overline{u}
,
T^{\prime}
must have tried to write
d
using the

rdcss
at line
31
.
The
rdcss
must have returned either
d
or the expected
value
{exp}_{i}
(it cannot return a descriptor different
from
d
, because lines
34
-
35
would be executed).
If the returned
value was the expected value
{exp}_{i}
, then the
rdcss
succeeded (since we
already know that
d
’s status was set to
Succ
by
T^{\prime}
later, and
d
’s
status is
Undec
previous to the change). If the
rdcss
at line
31

returned
d
, then some other thread (or
T^{\prime}
in a previous recursive

writeall
invocation) already wrote the descriptor. In other words,
in both cases, the descriptor was written. Since
d
was
written for each entry and its status pointer was set to
Succ

later, this corresponds to the successful case in

\left\llbracket{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\right\rrbracket
, which has as output
true
and the denotation is not empty.
∎

6
Related and Future Work

Proofs of RDCSS and MCAS

A number of papers have
considered linearizability of MCAS and RDCSS using LPs. For MCAS, the
PhD thesis of Vafeiadis
[
21
]
and the original paper of
Harris et al.
[
11
]
are the main sources for the LP proof.
For RDCSS, Vafeiadis’ PhD thesis proposed the use of
prophecy
variables

[
1
]
to model that the LPs of RDCSS depend on
future events. The argument was later mechanized in Coq by Liang and
Feng
[
18
]
using
speculations
(related to prophecy
variables, but less general), and eventually using prophecy variables
in Iris
[
15
]
.
Both speculations and prophecies lead to operational proofs, as they
essentially codify the LPs of the methods. Our proofs, being
declarative, do not rely on speculations or prophecies, but use spans
and visibility as the common abstractions that unify RDCSS and
MCAS. That said, we have not mechanized our proofs yet.

Visibility relations in other contexts

Our
approach uses visibility relations to model the ordering dependencies
between events, by axiomatizing the notions of observation and
separation. A general survey of the use of visibility relations in
concurrency and distributed systems is given by Viotti and
Vukolić
[
22
]
. Visibility relations and declarative proofs
have also been utilized to specify consistency criteria weaker than
linearizability (Emmi and Enea
[
8
]
), to introduce a
specification framework for weak memory models (Raad et
al.
[
20
]
), and to specify the RC11 memory model (Lahav et
al.
[
17
]
). In particular, for the RC11
model
[
17
]
, our observation relation is reminiscent of
the reads-from relation, and there may be a potential connection
between our separable-before and the (extended) coherence order in
RC11. However, the extended coherence order in RC11 is defined in
terms of reads-from, while in our approach, observation and
separable-before are independent abstract relations linked by the
visibility axioms.

In contrast to the above papers that focus on the semantics of
consistency criteria, our use of visibility relations focuses on
verifying specific algorithms and data structures, and is thus closer
to the following work where visibility relations are applied to
concurrent queues (Henzinger et
al.
[
13
,
6
]
), concurrent stacks (Dodds et
al.
[
7
]
), and memory snapshot algorithms (Öhman and
Nanevski
[
19
]
). We differ from these in the addressed
structures, RDCSS and MCAS, which, unlike the related work, further
utilize helping.

Alternative RDCSS and MCAS implementations

The
implementations of RDCSS and MCAS that we used here were originally
developed by Harris et al.
[
11
]
. However, there are
alternative implementations as well. For example, Arbel-Raviv and
Brown
[
3
]
present a variant of RDCSS where pointers to
descriptors are reused instead of being created at each RDCSS
invocation. Guerraoui et al.
[
10
]
present an MCAS
implementation that does not use RDCSS as a subroutine, and that uses
a minimal amount of CAS calls. Guerraoui et al. also survey a number
of other MCAS implementations. Feldman et
al.
[
9
]
present a wait-free implementation of MCAS
(the implementation we used here is only lock-free).

In the future, we will consider how our axioms apply to these
alternative implementations.
For example, our span axioms are agnostic in that they impose no
restriction on how many times a pointer to a descriptor can be used
across spans. Thus, we expect that our axiomatization will be able to
handle implementations with descriptor reuse, such as those of
Arbel-Raviv and Brown
[
3
]
. Similarly, we expect that we
could handle MCAS of Guerraoui et al.
[
10
]
, as it too
should satisfy the span axioms. Indeed, this implementation follows the
general outline of MCAS by writing the descriptor into all input
pointers before any descriptor resolution is attempted.

Observation and separation as a general methodology

The pattern suggested by Sections
3.1
and

3.2
, where
one starts with a history-based sequential specification and transforms
it into a concurrent specification by replacing the returns-before relation

\sqsubset
with a separable-before
relation
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
defined in terms of gaps
between events,
points towards a general methodology for obtaining visibility
relations for a given data structure.
We have also attempted the pattern on queues, and found that it does
derive a variant of the queue axioms of Henzinger et
al.
[
13
,
6
]
and applies to the queue of
Herlihy and Wing
[
14
]
. In the future, we plan to study
if this pattern applies to other concurrent data structures (e.g.,
memory snapshots, trees, lists, sets, etc.) and if the induced notions
of observation and separation are usable in more general settings
(e.g., to address other flavors of
helping
[
12
]
).

7
Conclusions

In this paper, we show that axiomatization of visibility relations are
a powerful alternative to linearization points, when it comes to
verifying linearizability of a number of helping algorithms with
common semantic structure. In particular, we applied the technique to
the descriptor-based algorithms RDCSS and MCAS, identifying and
axiomatizing the relations of separation and observation between
events. We achieved further abstraction by axiomatizing the notion of
spans to model how help requests bunch together in these algorithms,
and to allow expressing the separation and disjointness of the
bunches.

Appendix A
Proof of Linearizability for RDCSS and MCAS

A.1
Common definitions and results

This section has results that apply to
both the RDCSS and MCAS data structures.
In particular, the section
focuses on the first step of the linearization proof
(see gray box in Figure
1
), which
is proving the existence of the linear order.
Sections
A.2

and
A.3

then prove sequential soundness of the linear order
for RDCSS and MCAS, respectively.

Definition A.1

(Specification)
.

Given two pointer-indexed relations

{\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
(
observation relation
) and

{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Ev}
(
separable-before relation
), a

specification
, denoted

V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, is a triple

(\mathcal{W}_{p},\mathcal{A}_{p},\mathcal{Q})
consisting
of:

•

A pointer-indexed set
{\mathcal{W}_{p}}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}
(called
successful write events
).

•

A pointer-indexed set
{\mathcal{A}_{p}}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}
(called
alloc events
).

•

A predicate
{\mathcal{Q}}:\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{Val}\rightarrow\textsc{Bool}
(called
postcondition predicate
).
We denote by

\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}
the application of
\mathcal{Q}
to

x\in\textsc{Ev}
and
v\in\textsc{Val}
.

Given relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
, we say that
V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})=(\mathcal{W}_{p},\mathcal{A}_{p},\mathcal{Q})
is
valid
if all
the
visibility axioms
from Figure
11(b)
are satisfied.

General visibility relation

{\prec}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\bigcup_{p}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}\cup\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

Returns-before relation

{e}\sqsubset{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{e}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{e^{\prime}}.\textit{start}

Set of terminated events

T\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\mid{e}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\}

Closure of terminated events

\overline{T}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\{e\mid\exists t\in T.\ e\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}t\}

(a)

Defined notions for visibility axioms.

(
C_{1}
) No in-between

({w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{r}\wedge w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{p})\implies({w^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w}\vee{r}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w^{\prime}})

(
C_{2}
) Observed events are writes

{w}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{-}\implies w\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

(
C_{3}
) No future dependences

{x}\prec^{+}{y}\implies{y}\not\sqsubseteq{x}

(
C_{4}
) Return value completion

\exists v.\ \mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}\wedge(x\in T\implies v={x}.\textit{out})

(
C_{5}
) Allocation uniqueness

w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}\implies w=w^{\prime}

(
C_{6}
) Written pointers are allocated

w\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\implies\exists w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{p}.\ {w^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{p}}{w}

(b)

Visibility axioms for the validity of
V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}})=(\mathcal{W}_{p},\mathcal{A}_{p},\mathcal{Q})
.

Figure 11
:
Visibility Axioms. Variables
w
,
w^{\prime}
,
r
,
x
,
y
range over
\overline{T}
.
Variables
e
,
e^{\prime}
range over
Ev
. Variables
p
and
v
over
pts
and
Val
, respectively.

Section
3.2
already explained axioms
C_{\ref{help-focused::non-helpers}}
through
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
.
Here, we quickly mention the rest. Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::allocs}}
states that pointers are
created by at most one alloc. Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::all-writers-are-willing-helpers}}

states that for any successful
p
-write, there must
exist a
p
-alloc separable before the write. Intuitively, this just
expresses that input pointers must have been created by previous
allocs.

Definition A.2

(Section Hypotheses)
.

To shorten the statements of the lemmas and theorems, we will assume the following
hypotheses along this entire section,

•

Ev
is an arbitrary set of events.

•

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
and
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}

are two arbitrary pointer-indexed binary relations over
Ev
.

•

V(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

is an arbitrary valid specification.

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
Relation
\prec
is acyclic on
\overline{T}
.

Proof.

Suppose there is a cycle for some
x\in\overline{T}
.
Hence,
{x}\prec^{+}{x}
holds. But, by
Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::vis-acyclic}}
,

{x}\not\sqsubseteq{x}
must hold, which means

x\neq x
(Contradiction).
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
Relation
\sqsubseteq
is a partial order on
Ev
. In addition,
\sqsubset
satisfies the interval
order property,

\forall w,x,y,z\in\textsc{Ev}.\ ({w}\sqsubset{x}\wedge{y}\sqsubset{z})\rightarrow({w}\sqsubset{z}\vee{y}\sqsubset{x})

Proof.

To show that
\sqsubseteq
is a partial order,
it is enough to show that
\sqsubset

is irreflexive and transitive, because it is
a standard result that the reflexive closure of
an irreflexive and transitive relation is a partial order.

•

Irreflexivity. If
{x}\sqsubset{x}
, then we would
have
{x}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{start}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{end}
, which is a contradiction.

•

Transitivity. If
{x}\sqsubset{y}
and

{y}\sqsubset{z}
, then

{x}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{y}.\textit{start}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{y}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{z}.\textit{start}
, which means

{x}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{z}.\textit{start}
.
Hence,
{x}\sqsubset{z}
.

We now show that
\sqsubset
satisfies the
interval order property.

Let
{w}\sqsubset{x}
and
{y}\sqsubset{z}
.
Hence,
{w}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{start}

and
{y}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{z}.\textit{start}
.

Either
{w}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{z}.\textit{start}
or

{z}.\textit{start}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{w}.\textit{end}
. The first
case leads to
{w}\sqsubset{z}
.

For the second case, by using the hypotheses, we have

{y}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{z}.\textit{start}\leq_{\mathbb{N}}{w}.\textit{end}<_{\mathbb{N}}{x}.\textit{start}
.
Therefore,
{y}\sqsubset{x}
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
Let
n>_{\mathbb{N}}0
. If

a_{0}R_{1}a_{1}R_{2}\ldots R_{n-1}a_{n-1}R_{n}a_{n}

is a sequence of
n
steps where each
R_{i}
is either

\prec
or
\sqsubset
, and each
a_{i}\in\overline{T}
,
then
{a_{n}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
.

Proof.

By strong induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
. So, we have
a_{0}R_{1}a_{1}
.

If
R_{1}={\prec}
, then from

{a_{0}}\prec{a_{1}}
and Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::vis-acyclic}}
,
we get
{a_{1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
.

If
R_{1}={\sqsubset}
, then from

{a_{0}}\sqsubset{a_{1}}
, we cannot have

{a_{1}}\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
, because we would get

a_{0}\sqsubset a_{1}\sqsubseteq a_{0}
(Contradiction by Lemma
A.2
).

•

Inductive case. Let

a_{0}R_{1}a_{1}R_{2}\ldots R_{n}a_{n}R_{n+1}a_{n+1}
be a
sequence of
n+1
steps.

If all
R_{i}
are
\prec
, then

{a_{n+1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
follows by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::vis-acyclic}}
.

Therefore, we can assume that for some

1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
,
we have
R_{i}={\sqsubset}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\overbrace{a_{0}R_{1}\ldots R_{i-1}a_{i-1}}^{i-1\text{ steps}}\sqsubset\overbrace{a_{i}R_{i+1}\ldots R_{n+1}a_{n+1}}^{n-i+1\text{ steps}}

(6)

Suppose for a contradiction that

{a_{n+1}}\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
.

If
a_{n+1}=a_{0}
. Then,

a_{i}R_{i+1}\ldots R_{n}a_{n}R_{n+1}a_{0}R_{1}\ldots R_{i-1}a_{i-1}

is a sequence of
(n-i+1)+(i-1)=n
steps. Therefore,
by the inductive hypothesis,

{a_{i-1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{i}}
, which contradicts
(
6
).

It remains to check the case

{a_{n+1}}\sqsubset{a_{0}}
.

–

Subcase
i=1
. Hence,

{a_{n+1}}\sqsubset{a_{0}}\sqsubset a_{1}
.

But
{a_{1}}R_{2}\ldots R_{n+1}{a_{n+1}}
is a sequence
of
n
steps. Therefore, by
the inductive hypothesis,

{a_{n+1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{1}}
(Contradiction).

–

Subcase
i=n+1
. Hence,

{a_{n}}\sqsubset{a_{n+1}}\sqsubset a_{0}
.

But
{a_{0}}R_{1}\ldots R_{n}{a_{n}}
is a sequence
of
n
steps. Therefore, by
the inductive hypothesis,

{a_{n}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
(Contradiction).

–

Subcase
1<_{\mathbb{N}}i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
.

Since
{a_{i-1}}\sqsubset{a_{i}}
and

{a_{n+1}}\sqsubset{a_{0}}
, by Lemma

A.2
,
either
{a_{i-1}}\sqsubset{a_{0}}
or

{a_{n+1}}\sqsubset{a_{i}}
.

But
a_{0}R_{1}\ldots R_{i-1}a_{i-1}
is a sequence with

0<_{\mathbb{N}}i-1<_{\mathbb{N}}n

steps and

a_{i}R_{i+1}\ldots R_{n+1}a_{n+1}

is a sequence with

0<_{\mathbb{N}}n-i+1<_{\mathbb{N}}n
steps.
Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis,

{a_{i-1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{0}}
and

{a_{n+1}}\not\sqsubseteq{a_{i}}

(Contradiction).

∎

Definition A.3
.

Define the
happens-before
relation
{\vartriangleleft}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{(\prec\cup\sqsubset)^{+}}
, i.e.,
the transitive closure of the union of the general visibility and returns-before relations.

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
The relation
\vartriangleleft
is irreflexive on

\overline{T}
.

Proof.

If
{x}\vartriangleleft{x}
, then for some

n>_{\mathbb{N}}0
, there is a sequence

a_{0}R_{1}\ldots R_{n}a_{n}
of
n
steps, where
each
R_{i}
is either

\prec
or
\sqsubset
, each
a_{i}\in\overline{T}
and

a_{0}=x
, and
a_{n}=x
.

But by Lemma

A.3
,
we would have
{x}\not\sqsubseteq{x}

which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
The relation
\trianglelefteq
is a partial order on
\overline{T}
.

Proof.

It is a standard result that the reflexive closure of
an irreflexive and transitive relation is a partial order.
Hence,
\trianglelefteq
is a partial order on

\overline{T}
by Lemma

A.4
and
the fact that
\vartriangleleft
is transitive by definition.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
Set
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed. In other words,
if
x\in\overline{T}
and
{y}\prec{x}
, then
y\in\overline{T}
.

Proof.

Since
x\in\overline{T}
, there is
z\in T
such that

x\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}z
. So
y\prec x\mathrel{{\prec}^{*}}z

which means
y\in\overline{T}
.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
Given a partial order
\leq
over
\overline{T}

such that
{\trianglelefteq}\subseteq{\leq}
, the following holds,

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in\overline{T}.\ {x}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{y}\implies\neg\exists z\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\cap\overline{T}.\ x<z<y

(7)

Proof.

Let
{x}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{y}
. Suppose for a contradiction that
x<z<y

for some
z\in\mathcal{W}_{p}\cap\overline{T}
.

By Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::non-helpers}}
,
either
{y}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{z}
or

{z}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{x}
or
y=z
or
x=z
.

•

Case
{y}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{z}
.
Since
{\trianglelefteq}\subseteq{\leq}
, we have
y<z
.
But by hypothesis
z<y
(Contradiction).

•

Case
{z}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{x}
.
Since
{\trianglelefteq}\subseteq{\leq}
, we have

z<x
. But by hypothesis
x<z
(Contradiction).

•

Case
y=z
. This contradicts hypothesis
z<y
.

•

Case
x=z
. This contradicts hypothesis
x<z
.

∎

Lemma A.8
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.2
.
There is a linear order
\leq
over
\overline{T}
containing

\prec
and
\sqsubset
, such that property (
7
)
holds.

Proof.

Any finite partial order is contained in a linear order
[
16
]
.
Pick some linear order
\leq
containing
the partial order
\trianglelefteq
over
\overline{T}
.

By Lemma
A.7
,
property (
7
) holds.
∎

A.2
Linearizability Proof for the RDCSS Data Structure

This section focuses on proving, for the RDCSS data structure, sequential soundness
of the linear order built in Lemma
A.8
.
This section makes use of Definition
A.1
.

Definition A.4

(Exportable Procedures in the RDCSS Data Structure)
.

The following are the exportable procedures in the RDCSS Data Structure,

\textit{rdcss}(d:\textsc{rdesc})

(RDCSS for descriptor
d
)

\textit{ralloc}(v:\textsc{Val},\ k:\textsc{ptKind})

(Allocation of pointer kind
k\in\{\textsc{cptr},\textsc{dptr}\}
)

\textit{rCAS}(p:\textsc{dptr},\ e:\textsc{Val},\ n:\textsc{Val})

(CAS for data pointers)

\textit{rwrite}(p:\textsc{dptr},\ v:\textsc{Val})

(Write for data pointers)

\textit{rread}(p:\textsc{dptr})

(Read for data pointers)

\textit{rCAS-c}(p:\textsc{cptr},\ e:\textsc{Val},\ n:\textsc{Val})

(CAS for control pointers)

\textit{rwrite-c}(p:\textsc{cptr},\ v:\textsc{Val})

(Write for control pointers)

\textit{rread-c}(p:\textsc{cptr})

(Read for control pointers)

For each
x\in\textsc{Ev}
and pointer
p
, we define
the
writer predicate

\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
.

Definition A.5

(Writer Predicate for the RDCSS Data Structure)
.

The
writer predicate

\textsc{writer}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{pts}\times\textsc{Val}
,
denoted
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
, is defined by cases,

\displaystyle\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}

\displaystyle(\exists d.\ x=\textit{rdcss}(d)\wedge p={d}.pt_{2}\wedge v={d}.new_{2})\vee{}

\displaystyle(x=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)\wedge{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out})\vee{}

\displaystyle x=\textit{rCAS}(p,\_,v)\vee x=\textit{rwrite}(p,v)\vee{}

\displaystyle x=\textit{rCAS-c}(p,\_,v)\vee x=\textit{rwrite-c}(p,v)

Predicate
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
denotes the value
v

in
{x}.\textit{in}
that
x
will attempt to write into pointer
p
.

Notice that for the case of allocs,
writer

is defined only after the alloc has finished, since

writer
depends on the pointer generated
by the alloc. We only need to consider terminated allocs
because any procedure
x
requires in its input the pointers produced by allocs
at the moment
x
is invoked.

We now define the specification for the RDCSS data structure.

Definition A.6

(Specification for the RDCSS Data Structure)
.

Given relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
,
we define the specification for the RDCSS data structure, denoted

V^{\textsc{R}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, as follows,

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{A}_{p}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}x=\textit{ralloc}(\_,\_)\wedge{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out}

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{cases}p={d}.pt_{2}\wedge\exists z_{1},z_{2}.\ {z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{x}\wedge{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{x}\wedge{}&\text{if }x=\textit{rdcss}(d)\\
\quad\textsc{writer}\ {z_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {{d}.exp_{1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.exp_{2}}&\\
{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out}&\text{if }x=\textit{ralloc}(\_,\_)\\
p=q\wedge\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {e}&\text{if }x=\textit{rCAS}(q,e,\_)\\
p=q&\text{if }x=\textit{rwrite}(q,\_)\\
p=q\wedge\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {e}&\text{if }x=\textit{rCAS-c}(q,e,\_)\\
p=q&\text{if }x=\textit{rwrite-c}(q,\_)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{cases}\exists z_{2}.\ {z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v}\wedge{}&\text{if }x=\textit{rdcss}(d)\\
\quad(v={d}.exp_{2}\implies\exists z_{1}.\ {z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{x})&\\
{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge v={x}.\textit{out}&\text{if }x=\textit{ralloc}(\_,\_)\\
\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}&\text{if }x=\textit{rCAS}(q,\_,\_)\\
v=tt&\text{if }x=\textit{rwrite}(\_,\_)\\
\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}&\text{if }x=\textit{rread}(q)\\
\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}&\text{if }x=\textit{rCAS-c}(q,\_,\_)\\
v=tt&\text{if }x=\textit{rwrite-c}(\_,\_)\\
\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}&\text{if }x=\textit{rread-c}(q)\\
\end{cases}

We have the following immediate lemma,

Lemma A.9
.

If
x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
, then there is

v\in\textsc{Val}
such that
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
.

Proof.

By cases on
x
.

•

Case
x=\textit{rdcss}(d)
. We have
p={d}.pt_{2}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {{d}.new_{2}}
follows by definition of
writer
.

•

Case
x=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)
. We have

p={x}.\textit{out}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
follows by definition of
writer
.

•

Case
x=\textit{rCAS}(q,\_,n)
. We have

p=q
and

\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {n}
follows by definition of
writer
.

•

Case
x=\textit{rwrite}(q,v)
. We have

p=q
and

\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
follows by definition of
writer
.

•

Cases when
x
is one of
\textit{rCAS-c}(q,\_,n)
or
\textit{rwrite-c}(q,v)

are similar.

∎

Towards defining the notion of sequential soundness for a linear order over
events in RDCSS, we need to describe what it means for events to execute.
For that matter, we define the following state-based operational semantics,
so that intuitively, events execute by building
a path in the operational semantics.

Definition A.7

(Operational semantics for events in the RDCSS Data Structure)
.

Let
Op^{R}
denote the operational semantics generated by
the following base steps, where states are heaps and labels
are of the form “
proc(in)\ \langle out\rangle
”
where
proc
is the procedure name,
in
the procedure’s input, and
out
the procedure’s output.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \langle v\rangle}H
,
if
H({d}.pt_{2})=v
and
v\neq{d}.exp_{2}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \langle{d}.exp_{2}\rangle}H
,
if
H({d}.pt_{2})={d}.exp_{2}
and
H({d}.pt_{1})\neq{d}.exp_{1}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \langle{{d}.exp_{2}}\rangle}{H}[{{{d}.pt_{2}}\mapsto{{d}.new_{2}}}]
,
if
H({d}.pt_{2})={d}.exp_{2}
and
H({d}.pt_{1})={d}.exp_{1}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)\ \langle p\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]
,
if
p\notin dom(H)
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS}(p,e,\_)\ \langle v\rangle}H
,
if
H(p)=v
and
v\neq e
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS}(p,e,n)\ \langle e\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{n}}]
,
if
H(p)=e
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rwrite}(p,v)\ \langle tt\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]
,
if
p\in dom(H)
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rread}(p)\ \langle v\rangle}H
, if

H(p)=v
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS-c}(p,e,\_)\ \langle v\rangle}H
,
if
H(p)=v
and
v\neq e
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS-c}(p,e,n)\ \langle e\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{n}}]
,
if
H(p)=e
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rwrite-c}(p,v)\ \langle tt\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]
,
if
p\in dom(H)
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{rread-c}(p)\ \langle v\rangle}H
, if

H(p)=v
.

We now define the notion of sequential soundness of a linear order
\leq
.

Definition A.8

(Sequential soundness of a linear order)
.

Given a linear order
\leq
over a set of events, and a path
P
in the operational semantics
Op^{R}
,
we say that
P

matches

\leq
if for every step
i
in
P
,

•

The
i
-th procedure’s name in
P
equals the procedure’s name of the
i
-th event in
\leq
.

•

The
i
-th procedure’s input in
P
equals the procedure’s input of the
i
-th event in
\leq
.

•

If the
i
-th event in
\leq
is terminated, then the

i
-th procedure’s output in
P
equals the
i
-th event output in
\leq
.

We say that
\leq
is
sequentially sound
if there is a matching path in
Op^{R}
, starting from the empty heap and
having as many steps as the number of events in the domain of
\leq
.

Notation
.

If path
P
matches
\leq
, and
H\xrightarrow{proc(in)\ \langle out\rangle}H^{\prime}
is a step in
P
,
we denote by
\left\llbracket{proc}\right\rrbracket
the corresponding event in
\leq
.

To prove sequential soundness, we require two lemmas.
The first lemma, called writes
lemma, expresses that successful writes modify the heap
and as long as there are no successful
writes afterwards, such changes remain intact.
The second lemma, called allocs lemma, states that allocs create
pointers in the heap.

Definition A.9

(Section Hypotheses)
.

To shorten the statements of lemmas from
A.10

to
A.13
, the
following hypotheses will apply to those lemmas,

•

Ev
is an arbitrary set of events.

•

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
and
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}

are two arbitrary pointer-indexed binary relations over
Ev
.

•

V^{\textsc{R}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

is valid, where
V^{\textsc{R}}
is the specification for RDCSS defined in
A.6
.

We can now prove the writes lemma,

Lemma A.10

(Writes lemma)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.9
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a path of length
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
in
Op^{R}
that matches

\leq
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

Let
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.
If
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}

and for all
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
,
then
H_{n}(p)=v
.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
.

Suppose
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}
.

All non-alloc steps at
proc_{1}
require that some
pointer exists at
H_{0}=\emptyset
, which is impossible.
Therefore,
the only applicable step is when
proc_{1}
is an alloc.
Hence,
proc_{1}(in_{1})=\textit{ralloc}(v^{\prime},\_)

for some
v^{\prime}
.

But,

p={\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=out_{1}
, since

\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
,

proc_{1}
matches
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket
,
and
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in T
by definition of

\mathcal{W}_{p}
.
Also,
v^{\prime}=v
by definition of
writer
.

Therefore,
H_{1}(p)=v
,
since the alloc step creates
out_{1}=p
with initial value
v^{\prime}=v
.

•

Inductive case. Let
n\geq_{\mathbb{N}}1
and
suppose the statement holds for any path of length
n
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a matching path of length
n+1
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}\xrightarrow{proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})\ \langle out_{n+1}\rangle}H_{n+1}

Let
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n+1

and
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}

and for all
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
.
We need to show that
H_{n+1}(p)=v
.

–

Case
i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
, hence
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.
Since
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
is a subpath of length
n

that matches
\leq
, the inductive hypothesis concludes

H_{n}(p)=v
.

By hypothesis,

\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
must hold.
We do a case analysis on
proc_{n+1}
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rdcss}(d)
.
Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
, we have either

p\neq{d}.pt_{2}
or,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall z_{1},z_{2}.\ ({z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket})\implies{}\\
(\neg\textsc{writer}\ {z_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {{d}.exp_{1}}\vee\neg\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.exp_{2}})\end{split}

(8)

The case
p\neq{d}.pt_{2}
implies
H_{n+1}(p)=v

since any of the RDCSS steps do not change
pointers different from
{d}.pt_{2}
. We now focus on the case
(
8
).

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists z_{2}.\ {z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v^{\prime}}\wedge{}\\
(v^{\prime}={d}.exp_{2}\implies\exists z_{1}.\ {z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket})\end{split}

(9)

·

Case
v^{\prime}={d}.exp_{2}
. Hence, by
(
9
) and Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
,

z_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{1}}
holds. So, by Lemma

A.9
with

z_{1}
, and
(
8
) with
z_{1}
and

z_{2}
we have

\textsc{writer}\ {z_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {v^{\prime\prime}}
for some

v^{\prime\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{1}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.exp_{2}}
.

We know that
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
is a subpath
of length
n
that matches
\leq
, and
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
must
appear before
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket
in
\leq
,
since
\leq
contains
\prec
.
So, there are
proc_{l}
and
proc_{m}
such that
l,m\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
and

\left\llbracket{proc_{l}}\right\rrbracket=z_{1}
and

\left\llbracket{proc_{m}}\right\rrbracket=z_{2}
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
and
property (
7
),
we also have the following facts:

z_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{1}}
,

z_{2}\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}
,
and for all
l<_{\mathbb{N}}j\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{1}}
,
and for all
m<_{\mathbb{N}}j\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}
.

Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis applied twice on the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]

for
l
and
m
, we must have

H_{n}({d}.pt_{1})=v^{\prime\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{1}
and
H_{n}({d}.pt_{2})={d}.exp_{2}
.

Hence,
proc_{n+1}
must be a failing
rdcss
step in the operational semantics.
This means that if either
p={d}.pt_{2}

or not, we will have
H_{n+1}(p)=v
,
since
H_{n}(p)=v
and the
n+1
step does not modify pointers.

·

Case
v^{\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{2}
. From (
9
),
{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}
and
\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v^{\prime}}
hold.

By a similar argument as the previous case,
z_{2}
must be one of the
procedures in the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
.
Therefore, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
, property (
7
),
and the inductive hypothesis,
H_{n}({d}.pt_{2})=v^{\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{2}
must hold.

Hence,
proc_{n+1}
must be a failing
rdcss
step in the operational semantics.
This means that if either
p={d}.pt_{2}

or not, we will have
H_{n+1}(p)=v
,
since
H_{n}(p)=v
and the
n+1
step does not modify pointers.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)
.
Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
,
we have,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}\neq\bot\implies p\neq{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}

But by Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}\neq\bot
.
Hence,
p\neq{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}
.

But since
proc_{n+1}
matches
\leq
and

\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket
has terminated,
we also have
{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=out_{n+1}
.
Therefore,
p\neq out_{n+1}
.

Since the alloc step only creates
out_{n+1}
, pointer
p
remains
unmodified, hence
H_{n+1}(p)=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rCAS}(r,e,m)
.
Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
, we have either

p\neq r
or,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{r}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\implies\neg\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {r}\ {e}\end{split}

(10)

The case
p\neq r
implies
H_{n+1}(p)=v

since the
rCAS
steps do not change
pointers different from
r
. We now focus on the case
(
10
).

By Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{r}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {r}\ {v^{\prime}}

(11)

Hence, by
(
11
) and
(
10
) with
z
,
we have
v^{\prime}\neq e
.

By a similar argument as the
rdcss
case,
z
must be one of the
procedures in the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
.
Therefore, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
, property (
7
),
and the inductive hypothesis,
H_{n}(r)=v^{\prime}\neq e
must hold.

Hence,
proc_{n+1}
must be a failing
rCAS
step in the operational semantics.
This means that if either
p=r

or not, we will have
H_{n+1}(p)=v
,
since
H_{n}(p)=v
and the
n+1
step does not modify pointers.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rwrite}(r,m)
.
By definition, we have
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{r}
.
Therefore,
p\neq r
(since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
by hypothesis), which means
H_{n+1}(p)=v

since the
rwrite
step does not change
pointers different from
r
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rread}(r)
.
We have
H_{n+1}(p)=v
since the
rread
step does not change pointers.

*

Cases for
rCAS-c
,
rwrite-c
, and
rread-c

are identical to the cases for
rCAS
,
rwrite
, and
rread
,
respectively.

–

Case
i=n+1
. So, we have
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}
.
We do a case analysis on

proc_{n+1}
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rdcss}(d)
.

Hence,
p={d}.pt_{2}
(by definition of
\mathcal{W}_{p}
),

v={d}.new_{2}
(by definition of
writer
), and,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists z_{1},z_{2}.\ {z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge{}\\
\textsc{writer}\ {z_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {{d}.exp_{1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {{d}.exp_{2}}\end{split}

By a similar argument as the
rdcss
case when
i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
,
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
must be one of the
procedures in the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
.
Therefore, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
, property (
7
),
and the inductive hypothesis,
H_{n}({d}.pt_{1})={d}.exp_{1}
and
H_{n}({d}.pt_{2})={d}.exp_{2}
must hold.

Therefore, the
proc_{n+1}
step
must be the successful
rdcss
step, which means

H_{n+1}={H_{n}}[{{{d}.pt_{2}}\mapsto{{d}.new_{2}}}]
.
Hence
H_{n+1}(p)=H_{n+1}({d}.pt_{2})={d}.new_{2}=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{ralloc}(v^{\prime},\_)
.
Hence,
p={\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}

and
{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{end}\neq\bot

(by definition of
\mathcal{W}_{p}
).
Hence,
v=v^{\prime}
(by definition of
writer
).

Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket
has terminated
and
proc_{n+1}
matches
\leq
,
{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=out_{n+1}
.
Hence,
out_{n+1}=p
.

But the alloc step implies
H_{n+1}={H_{n}}[{{out_{n+1}}\mapsto{v^{\prime}}}]
,
which means
H_{n+1}(p)=H_{n+1}(out_{n+1})=v^{\prime}=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rCAS}(q,e,m)
.

By definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{p}
and
writer
,
p=q
,
v=m
, and,

\displaystyle\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {e}

So,
z
must be one of the procedures in the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
.
Therefore, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
, property (
7
),
and the inductive hypothesis,
H_{n}(q)=e
must hold.

Therefore, the
proc_{n+1}
step
must be the successful
rCAS
step, which means

H_{n+1}={H_{n}}[{{q}\mapsto{e}}]
.
Hence
H_{n+1}(p)=H_{n+1}(q)=e=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rwrite}(q,m)
.
By definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{p}
and
writer
,
p=q
and
v=m
.

The only applicable transition for the
p_{n+1}
step
is the
rwrite
step, which means

H_{n+1}={H_{n}}[{{q}\mapsto{m}}]
.
Hence
H_{n+1}(p)=H_{n+1}(q)=m=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rread}(q)
.
This case is impossible, since reads are not elements of

\mathcal{W}_{p}
.

*

Cases for
rCAS-c
,
rwrite-c
, and
rread-c

are identical to the cases for
rCAS
,
rwrite
, and
rread
,
respectively.

∎

Lemma A.11

(Allocs lemma)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.9
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a path of length
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
in
Op^{R}
that matches

\leq
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

Then,
p\in dom(H_{n})
if and only if
there is
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
,
such that
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
.

–

\Rightarrow
. Let
p\in dom(H_{1})
.
Procedure
proc_{1}
must be an alloc,
since all other steps preserve the
heap domain (i.e. it would be the case that

p\in dom(H_{0})=\emptyset
for non-alloc steps, which
is impossible).
Hence,
proc_{1}(in_{1})=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)
for some
v
,
and
out_{1}=p
, since
p
is in the only pointer in the domain of
H_{1}
.

By Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
,
and since the path matches
\leq
,
p=out_{1}={\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}
.
Therefore,
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
by definition of

\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

–

\Leftarrow
. Suppose

\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
. Therefore,

p={\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}
,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
,
and
proc_{1}=\textit{ralloc}
.

Since the path matches
\leq
,
out_{1}={\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=p
.
Therefore, the step creates pointer
p
and
p\in dom(H_{1})
.

•

Inductive case. Let
n\geq_{\mathbb{N}}1
and
suppose the statement holds for any path of length
n
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a matching path of length
n+1
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}\xrightarrow{proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})\ \langle out_{n+1}\rangle}H_{n+1}

–

\Rightarrow
. Let
p\in dom(H_{n+1})
. We consider the following cases,

*

Case
p\in dom(H_{n})
. By the inductive
hypothesis on the subpath
[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}]
,
there is
proc_{i}
such that
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n

(hence,
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
) such that

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

*

Case
p\notin dom(H_{n})
. Procedure

proc_{n+1}
must be an alloc, since the rest
of the steps in the operational semantics preserve the heap domain,
and we know that
p\in dom(H_{n+1})
.
Hence,
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)
for some
v
,
and
out_{n+1}=p
, since an alloc step adds a single pointer to a heap and

p
is a new pointer in the domain of
H_{n+1}
.

By Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
,
and since the path matches
\leq
, we have
p=out_{n+1}={\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}
.
Therefore,
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
by definition of

\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

–

\Leftarrow
. Suppose there is
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
such
that
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

For the case
i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
(hence,
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
), the inductive
hypothesis on the subpath
[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}]
concludes
p\in dom(H_{n})
. But the fact that
steps in the operational semantics do not remove pointers implies that

p\in dom(H_{n+1})
.

We now check the case
i=n+1
.
Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
,
we have
p={\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}
,

{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
,
and
proc_{n+1}=\textit{ralloc}
.

Since the path matches
\leq
, we have
out_{n+1}={\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=p
.
Therefore, the step creates pointer
p
and
p\in dom(H_{n+1})
.

∎

With the writes and alloc lemmas, we can now prove the existence of a path.

Lemma A.12

(Path Existence)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.9
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
For any
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}\lvert\overline{T}\rvert
,
there is a path in
Op^{R}
of length
n
that matches
\leq
and starts from the empty heap.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
. Denote by
x_{1}
the first event in
\leq
.

If
x_{1}
is any of

rdcss
,
rCAS
,
rread
,
rCAS-c
, and

rread-c
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

and the definition of the postcondition predicate force these events
to observe another event in at least one pointer. For example,

\textit{rdcss}(d)
observes a
z_{2}
in pointer
{d}.pt_{2}
, and

\textit{rCAS}(q,e,n)
observes a
z
in pointer
q
.
These observed events (like
z_{2}
and
z
)
must be in
\overline{T}
because
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
A.6
).
Therefore, there must exist an event occurring
before

x_{1}
in
\leq
, which is impossible.

Hence,
x_{1}
must be either a
rwrite
,
rwrite-c
, or

ralloc
. However, if
x_{1}
is either

rwrite
or
rwrite-c
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::all-writers-are-willing-helpers}}

forces the existence of an alloc
z
such that
{z}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{x_{1}}
for some

p
(equality cannot hold because
x_{1}
is not an alloc).
This
z
must be in
\overline{T}
because
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
A.6
).
In other words,
z
must occur
before

x_{1}
in
\leq
, which
is impossible.

Hence,
x_{1}
must be an alloc of the form
\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)

for some
v
.

Now, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

concludes
{x_{1}}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
. Define
q\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{x_{1}}.\textit{out}
,
and
H_{1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{\emptyset}[{{q}\mapsto{v}}]
.
Then,
\emptyset\xrightarrow{\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)\ \left\langle q\right\rangle}H_{1}
is a path
of length
1
starting from the empty heap that matches
\leq
.

•

Inductive case. Let
n\geq_{\mathbb{N}}1
. Suppose

n+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
.
We need to show that there is a matching path of length
n+1
.
Since
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
,
the inductive hypothesis implies that there is a matching path of length
n
,

\displaystyle H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

(12)

We need to show that we can extend this path with a matching
n+1
step
for the
n+1
event in
\leq
. Denote the
n+1
event in
\leq
as
x_{n+1}
.

We do a case analysis on
x_{n+1}
,

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{rdcss}(d)
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists z_{2}.\ {z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{x_{n+1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z_{2}}\ {{d}.pt_{2}}\ {v}\wedge{}\\
(v={d}.exp_{2}\implies\exists z_{1}.\ {z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{x_{n+1}})\end{split}

(13)

Since path (
12
)
matches
\leq
, (
13
) implies
that event
z_{2}
must be one of the procedures in the path,
since it occurs before
x_{n+1}
in
\leq
,
i.e.
z_{2}=\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket
for some
i
.

Notice that by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}
.
Also, for every
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{2}}

by property (
7
), since

{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{x_{n+1}}
.
Therefore, by Lemma

A.10

applied on path
(
12
), we have

H_{n}({d}.pt_{2})=v
.

We now case analyze
v
.

*

Case
v={d}.exp_{2}
. By
(
13
),

{z_{1}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{1}}}{x_{n+1}}
.
Hence, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
,

z_{1}\in\mathcal{W}_{{d}.pt_{1}}
.
By Lemma
A.9
,

\textsc{writer}\ {z_{1}}\ {{d}.pt_{1}}\ {v^{\prime}}
for some
v^{\prime}
.

By a similar argument as for

{z_{2}}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{d}.pt_{2}}}{x_{n+1}}
above, we will have

H_{n}({d}.pt_{1})=v^{\prime}
.

We now case analyze
v^{\prime}
.

·

Case
v^{\prime}={d}.exp_{1}
. Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{H_{n}}[{{{d}.pt_{2}}\mapsto{{d}.new_{2}}}]
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
consisting on the successful
rdcss
step, since
v={d}.exp_{2}
and
v^{\prime}={d}.exp_{1}
.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

·

Case
v^{\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{1}
. Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}H_{n}
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
consisting on a failing
rdcss
step, since
v={d}.exp_{2}
but
v^{\prime}\neq{d}.exp_{1}
.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

*

Case
v\neq{d}.exp_{2}
. Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}H_{n}
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rdcss}(d)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
consisting on a failing
rdcss
step, since
v\neq{d}.exp_{2}
.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)
.

By Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
,
{x_{n+1}}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
.
Define
q\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
.

We claim
q\notin dom(H_{n})
. For suppose

q\in dom(H_{n})
. Then, by Lemma

A.11
applied
on path (
12
), there is

proc_{i}
such that
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
and

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
, which means

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket<x_{n+1}
since
the path matches
\leq
.
But by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::allocs}}
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket=x_{n+1}
(Contradiction).

Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{H_{n}}[{{q}\mapsto{v}}]
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{ralloc}(v,\_)\ \left\langle q\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid
alloc step that matches
\leq
.

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{rCAS}(q,e,m)
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v
,

\displaystyle\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x_{n+1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}

By a similar argument as in the
rdcss
case,

H_{n}(q)=v
.

We now case analyze
v
.

*

Case
v=e
. Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{H_{n}}[{{q}\mapsto{m}}]
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS}(q,e,m)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}

is a valid step corresponding to the successful
rCAS
step.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

*

Case
v\neq e
. Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}H_{n}
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rCAS}(q,e,m)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
corresponding to the failing
rCAS
step.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{rwrite}(q,m)
.

By definition, we have
x_{n+1}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
.
Hence, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::all-writers-are-willing-helpers}}

there is
z\in\mathcal{A}_{q}
such that

{z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\phantom{$\ltimes$}}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$\ltimes$}\vskip-1.50696pt\hbox{$-$}}}}_{{q}}{x_{n+1}}
.
Case
z=x_{n+1}
cannot hold, since
x_{n+1}\notin\mathcal{A}_{q}

by definition of
\mathcal{A}_{q}
. Hence,

{z}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{q}}{x_{n+1}}
must be true.
In addition,
z
must be in
\overline{T}
because
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
A.6
).

Since the path matches
\leq
,
z
must be one of the procedures
in the path, i.e.,
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket=z
for some

i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.
By Lemma
A.11

applied on path
(
12
),

q\in dom(H_{n})
holds.

Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{H_{n}}[{{q}\mapsto{m}}]
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rwrite}(q,m)\ \left\langle tt\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
corresponding to the
rwrite
step.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
{x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}=tt
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{rread}(q)
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v
,

\displaystyle\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x_{n+1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}

By a similar argument as in the previous cases,

H_{n}(q)=v
.

Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}H_{n}
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{rread}(q)\ \left\langle v\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid step
corresponding to the
rread
step.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes
v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
. Hence, the step
matches
\leq
.

–

Cases for
rCAS-c
,
rwrite-c
, and
rread-c

are identical to the cases for
rCAS
,
rwrite
, and
rread
,
respectively.

∎

Lemma A.13

(Sequential Soundness)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.9
.
The linear order
\leq
of Lemma
A.8
is sequentially sound.

Proof.

If
\overline{T}=\emptyset
, then the empty path matches
\leq
.
If
\overline{T}\neq\emptyset
, then Lemma
A.12

applied with
n=|\overline{T}|
ensures the existence of a matching path
for
\leq
.
∎

Theorem A.1
.

Given an implementation of the RDCSS, suppose that for any set of
abstract events
Ev
generated from an arbitrary execution
history in the implementation, there are relations

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
such that

V^{\textsc{R}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}})
is valid. Then, the
implementation is linearizable.

Proof.

Let
Ev
be a set of events generated from an arbitrary
execution history in the implementation.
From the hypothesis,
V^{\textsc{R}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},{\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}})
is valid
for some
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
and

\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma

A.8
. Then, we take the general
visibility relation
\prec
and
\leq
to be the
relations required by the definition of linearizability.
By construction,
\leq
respects both
\prec
and

\sqsubset
. Also,

\leq
is sequentially sound by Lemma

A.13
.
∎

A.3
Linearizability Proof for the MCAS Data Structure

This section focuses on proving, for the MCAS data structure, sequential soundness
of the linear order built in Lemma
A.8
.
This section makes use of Definition
A.1
.

Definition A.10

(Exportable Procedures in the MCAS Data Structure)
.

The following are the exportable procedures in the MCAS Data Structure,

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u}:\textsc{list}\,\textsc{update\_entry})

(MCAS for list of entries
\overline{u}\neq\emptyset
)

\textit{malloc}(v:\textsc{Val})

(Allocation of pointer)

\textit{mwrite}(p:\textsc{dptr},\ v:\textsc{Val})

(Write procedure)

\textit{mread}(p:\textsc{dptr})

(Read procedure)

As we did for the RDCSS data structure, we define the writer predicate,

Definition A.11

(Writer Predicate for the MCAS Data Structure)
.

The
writer predicate

\textsc{writer}\subseteq\textsc{Ev}\times\textsc{pts}\times\textsc{Val}
,
denoted
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
, is defined by cases,

\displaystyle\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}

\displaystyle(\exists\overline{u},i\in\overline{u}.\ x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\wedge v={new}_{i}\wedge p={pt}_{i})\vee{}

\displaystyle(x=\textit{malloc}(v)\wedge{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out})\vee{}

\displaystyle x=\textit{mwrite}(p,v)

Predicate
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
returns the value
v

in
{x}.\textit{in}
that
x
will attempt to write into pointer
p
.

We now define the specification for the MCAS data structure.

Definition A.12

(Specification for the MCAS Data Structure)
.

Given relations
\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
,
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}
,
we define the specification for the MCAS data structure, denoted

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
, as follows,

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{A}_{p}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}x=\textit{malloc}(\_)\wedge{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out}

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{cases}(\exists j\in\overline{u}.\ p={pt}_{j})\wedge(\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {{exp}_{i}})&\text{if }x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\\
{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge p={x}.\textit{out}&\text{if }x=\textit{malloc}(\_)\\
p=q&\text{if }x=\textit{mwrite}(q,\_)\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{{x},{v}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}\begin{cases}v\in\textsc{Bool}\wedge{}&\text{if }x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\\
\quad\begin{cases}\forall i\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {{exp}_{i}}&\text{if }v=true\\
\begin{aligned} \exists i\in\overline{u},v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Val}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{i}}}{x}\wedge{}\\
\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{i}}\ {v^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime}\neq{{exp}_{i}}\end{aligned}&\text{if }v=false\\
\end{cases}&\\
{x}.\textit{end}\neq\bot\wedge v={x}.\textit{out}&\text{if }x=\textit{malloc}(\_)\\
v=tt&\text{if }x=\textit{mwrite}(\_,\_)\\
\exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{q}}{x}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {q}\ {v}&\text{if }x=\textit{mread}(q)\\
\end{cases}

We have the following immediate lemma,

Lemma A.14
.

If
x\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
, then there is

v\in\textsc{Val}
such that
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
.

Proof.

By cases on
x
.

•

Case
x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
. We have
p={pt}_{j}
for some

j\in\overline{u}
. And
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {{new}_{j}}
follows by definition of
writer
.

•

Cases when
x
is one of
\textit{malloc}(v)
or
\textit{mwrite}(q,v)
are as in the proof of Lemma

A.9
.

∎

Towards defining the notion of sequential soundness for a linear order over
events in MCAS, we need to describe what it means for events to execute.
For that matter, we define the following state-based operational semantics,
so that intuitively, events execute by building
a path in the operational semantics.

Definition A.13

(Operational semantics for events in the MCAS Data Structure)
.

Let
Op^{M}
denote the operational semantics generated by
the following base steps, where states are heaps and labels
are of the form “
proc(in)\ \langle out\rangle
”
where
proc
is the procedure name,
in
the procedure’s input, and
out
the procedure’s output.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle false\rangle}H
,
if for some
j\in\overline{u}
,
H({pt}_{j})\neq{exp}_{j}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \langle true\rangle}{H}[{{{pt}_{j}}\mapsto{{new}_{j}}}]_{j\in\overline{u}}
,
if for every
j\in\overline{u}
,
H({pt}_{j})={exp}_{j}
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{malloc}(v)\ \langle p\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]
,
if
p\notin dom(H)
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mwrite}(p,v)\ \langle tt\rangle}{H}[{{p}\mapsto{v}}]
,
if
p\in dom(H)
.

•

H\xrightarrow{\textit{mread}(p)\ \langle v\rangle}H
,
if
H(p)=v
.

We now define the notion of sequential soundness of a linear order
\leq
.

Definition A.14

(Sequential soundness of a linear order)
.

Given a linear order
\leq
over a set of events, and a path
P
in the operational semantics
Op^{M}
,
we say that
P

matches

\leq
if for every step
i
in
P
,

•

The
i
-th procedure’s name in
P
equals the procedure’s name of the
i
-th event in
\leq
.

•

The
i
-th procedure’s input in
P
equals the procedure’s input of the
i
-th event in
\leq
.

•

If the
i
-th event in
\leq
is terminated, then the

i
-th procedure’s output in
P
equals the
i
-th event output in
\leq
.

We say that
\leq
is
sequentially sound
if there is a matching path in
Op^{M}
, starting from the empty heap and
having as many steps as the number of events in the domain of
\leq
.

Notation
.

If path
P
matches
\leq
, and
H\xrightarrow{proc(in)\ \langle out\rangle}H^{\prime}
is a step in
P
,
we denote by
\left\llbracket{proc}\right\rrbracket
the corresponding event in
\leq
.

To prove sequential soundness, we require two lemmas.
The first lemma, called writes
lemma, expresses that successful writes modify the heap
and as long as there are no successful
writes afterwards, such changes remain intact.
The second lemma, called allocs lemma, states that allocs create
pointers in the heap.

Definition A.15

(Section Hypotheses)
.

To shorten the statements of lemmas from
A.15

to
A.18
, the
following hypotheses will apply to those lemmas,

•

Ev
is an arbitrary set of events.

•

\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}
and
\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}

are two arbitrary pointer-indexed binary relations over
Ev
.

•

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})

is valid, where
V^{\textsc{M}}
is the specification for MCAS defined in
A.12
.

We can now prove the writes lemma,

Lemma A.15

(Writes lemma)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.15
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a path of length
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
in
Op^{M}
that matches

\leq
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

Let
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.
If
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}

and for all
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
,
then
H_{n}(p)=v
.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
.

Suppose
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}
.

All non-alloc steps at
proc_{1}
in the operational semantics
(including successful
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
since
\overline{u}
is not empty)
require that some
pointer exists at
H_{0}=\emptyset
, which is impossible.
Therefore,
the only applicable case is when
proc_{1}
is an alloc.
Hence,
proc_{1}(in_{1})=\textit{ralloc}(v^{\prime},\_)

for some
v^{\prime}
.

But
p={\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket}.\textit{out}=out_{1}
, since

\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
,

proc_{1}
matches
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket
,
and
\left\llbracket{proc_{1}}\right\rrbracket\in T
by definition of

\mathcal{W}_{p}
.
Also,
v^{\prime}=v
by definition of
writer
.

Therefore,
H_{1}(p)=v
,
since the alloc step creates
out_{1}=p
with initial value
v^{\prime}=v
.

•

Inductive case. Let
n\geq_{\mathbb{N}}1
and
suppose the statement holds for any path of length
n
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a matching path of length
n+1
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}\xrightarrow{proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})\ \langle out_{n+1}\rangle}H_{n+1}

Let
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n+1

and
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}

and for all
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
.
We need to show that
H_{n+1}(p)=v
.

–

Case
i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
, hence
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.
Since
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
is a subpath of length
n

that matches
\leq
, the inductive hypothesis concludes

H_{n}(p)=v
.

By hypothesis,

\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
must hold.
We do a case analysis on
proc_{n+1}
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.
Since
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{p}
, we have either

\forall k\in\overline{u}.\ p\neq{pt}_{k}
or,
for some
m\in\overline{u}
,

\displaystyle\forall z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{m}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\implies\neg\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{m}}\ {{exp}_{m}}

(14)

The case
\forall k\in\overline{u}.\ p\neq{pt}_{k}
implies

H_{n+1}(p)=v

since the
mcas
steps only change pointers in
the set
\{{pt}_{k}\mid k\in\overline{u}\}
.
We now focus on the case
(
14
).

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Bool}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall k\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {{exp}_{k}}&\text{if }v^{\prime}=true\\
\begin{aligned} \exists k\in\overline{u},v^{\prime\prime}\in\textsc{Val}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge{}\\
\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {v^{\prime\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime\prime}\neq{{exp}_{k}}\end{aligned}&\text{if }v^{\prime}=false\\
\end{cases}

(15)

If
v^{\prime}=true
, by applying (
15
)
with
m
, it would contradict
(
14
).
Therefore,
v^{\prime}=false
. Hence, for some
k\in\overline{u}
,
z
and
v^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle{z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {v^{\prime\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime\prime}\neq{{exp}_{k}}

(16)

So, by Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
,

z\in\mathcal{W}_{{pt}_{k}}
.

We know that
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
is a subpath
of length
n
that matches
\leq
, and
z
must
appear before
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket
in
\leq
,
since
\leq
contains
\prec
.
So, there is
proc_{l}
such that
\left\llbracket{proc_{l}}\right\rrbracket=z
,
for some
l\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
and
property (
7
),
we also have the following facts:

z\in\mathcal{W}_{{pt}_{k}}
,
and for all
l<_{\mathbb{N}}j\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{{pt}_{k}}
.

Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis applied on the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]

and with
proc_{l}
, we must have
H_{n}({pt}_{k})=v^{\prime\prime}
, where
v^{\prime\prime}\neq{exp}_{k}
.

Hence,
proc_{n+1}
must be a failing
mcas
step in the operational semantics.
This means that if either
p={pt}_{k}

or not, we will have
H_{n+1}(p)=v
,
since
H_{n}(p)=v
and the
n+1
step does not modify pointers.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{malloc}(v)
.
Identical to the case for
ralloc
in Lemma
A.10
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{mwrite}(r,m)
.
Identical to the case for
rwrite
in Lemma
A.10
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{rread}(r)
.
Identical to the case for
rread
in Lemma
A.10
.

–

Case
i=n+1
. So, we have
\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{p}
and

\textsc{writer}\ {\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\ {p}\ {v}
.
We do a case analysis on

proc_{n+1}
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.

By definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{p}

and
writer
,
we have for some
l\in\overline{u}
,
p={pt}_{l}
,

v={new}_{l}
, and

\displaystyle\forall k\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{\left\llbracket{proc_{n+1}}\right\rrbracket}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {{exp}_{k}}

We claim that for every
k\in\overline{u}
,

H_{n}({pt}_{k})={exp}_{k}
.

Let
k\in\overline{u}
.

By a similar argument as the case for
i<_{\mathbb{N}}n+1
, event
z
must be one of the
procedures in the subpath
\left[proc_{1},\ldots,proc_{n}\right]
.
Therefore, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
, property (
7
),
and the inductive hypothesis,
H_{n}({pt}_{k})={exp}_{k}
must hold.

This proves the claim.

Therefore, the
proc_{n+1}
step
must be the successful
mcas
step, which means

H_{n+1}({pt}_{k})={new}_{k}
, for every
k\in\overline{u}
.
In particular,

H_{n+1}(p)=H_{n+1}({pt}_{l})={new}_{l}=v
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{malloc}(v)
.
Identical to the case for
ralloc
in Lemma
A.10
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{mwrite}(q,m)
.
Identical to the case for
rwrite
in Lemma
A.10
.

*

Case
proc_{n+1}(in_{n+1})=\textit{mread}(q)
.
Identical to the case for
rread
in Lemma
A.10
.

∎

Lemma A.16

(Allocs lemma)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.15
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a path of length
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
in
Op^{M}
that matches

\leq
,

H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

Then,
p\in dom(H_{n})
if and only if
there is
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
,
such that
\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{A}_{p}
.

Proof.

Identical to the proof of Lemma

A.11
, but using
malloc
instead of
ralloc
.
∎

Lemma A.17

(Path Existence)
.

Suppose hypotheses
A.15
.
Let
\leq
be the linear order of Lemma
A.8
.
For any
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}\lvert\overline{T}\rvert
,
there is a path in
Op^{M}
of length
n
that matches
\leq
and starts from the empty heap.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

•

Case
n=1
. Denote by
x_{1}
the first event in
\leq
.

If
x_{1}
is any of

mread
and
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
(since
\overline{u}
is not empty),
Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

and the definition of the postcondition predicate force these events
to observe another event in at least one pointer. For example,

\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
observes a
z
in every pointer in the non-empty list
\overline{u}
, and

\textit{mread}(q)
observes a
z
in pointer
q
.
This
z
must be in
\overline{T}
because
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
A.6
).
Therefore, there must exist an event occurring
before

x_{1}
in
\leq
, which is impossible.

Hence,
x_{1}
must be either a
mwrite
or

malloc
. However, if
x_{1}
is

mwrite
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::all-writers-are-willing-helpers}}

forces the existence of an alloc
z
such that
{z}\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}}{x_{1}}
for some

p
(equality cannot hold because
x_{1}
is not an alloc).
This
z
must be in
\overline{T}
because
\overline{T}
is
\prec
-downward closed
(Lemma
A.6
).
In other words,
z
must occur
before

x_{1}
in
\leq
, which
is impossible.

Hence,
x_{1}
must be an alloc of the form
\textit{malloc}(v)
for some
v
.
Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

concludes
{x_{1}}.\textit{end}\neq\bot
. Define
q\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{x_{1}}.\textit{out}
,
and
H_{1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{\emptyset}[{{q}\mapsto{v}}]
.
Then,
\emptyset\xrightarrow{\textit{malloc}(v)\ \left\langle q\right\rangle}H_{1}
is a path
of length
1
starting from the empty heap that matches
\leq
.

•

Inductive case. Let
n\geq_{\mathbb{N}}1
. Suppose

n+1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
.
We need to show that there is a matching path of length
n+1
.
Since
1\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n\leq_{\mathbb{N}}|\overline{T}|
,
the inductive hypothesis implies that there is a matching path of length
n
,

\displaystyle H_{0}=\emptyset\xrightarrow{proc_{1}(in_{1})\ \langle out_{1}\rangle}H_{1}\xrightarrow{proc_{2}(in_{2})\ \langle out_{2}\rangle}\ldots\xrightarrow{proc_{n}(in_{n})\ \langle out_{n}\rangle}H_{n}

(17)

We need to show that we can extend this path with a matching
n+1
step
for the
n+1
event in
\leq
. Denote the
n+1
event in
\leq
as
x_{n+1}
.

We do a case analysis on
x_{n+1}
,

–

Case
x_{n+1}=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.

By Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}
, we have for some
v\in\textsc{Bool}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall k\in\overline{u}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{x_{n+1}}\wedge\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {{exp}_{k}}&\text{if }v=true\\
\begin{aligned} \exists k\in\overline{u},v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Val}.\ \exists z.\ {z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{x_{n+1}}\wedge{}\\
\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {v^{\prime}}\wedge v^{\prime}\neq{{exp}_{k}}\end{aligned}&\text{if }v=false\\
\end{cases}

(18)

*

Case
v=true
.

We claim that for any
k\in\overline{u}
,

H_{n}({pt}_{k})={exp}_{k}
.

Let
k\in\overline{u}
. By (
18
)
there is
z
such that
{z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{x_{n+1}}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {{exp}_{k}}
.
Since path (
17
) matches
\leq

and
z
occurs before
x_{n+1}
in
\leq
,
we have

z=\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket
for some
i\leq_{\mathbb{N}}n
.

In addition, by Axiom

C_{\ref{help-focused::helped-are-writers}}
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{i}}\right\rrbracket\in\mathcal{W}_{{pt}_{k}}
.
Also, for every
j>_{\mathbb{N}}i
,

\left\llbracket{proc_{j}}\right\rrbracket\notin\mathcal{W}_{{pt}_{k}}

by property (
7
), since

{z}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{{pt}_{k}}}{x_{n+1}}
.
Therefore, by Lemma

A.15
with
path (
17
) and

\textsc{writer}\ {z}\ {{pt}_{k}}\ {{exp}_{k}}
,
we have
H_{n}({pt}_{k})={exp}_{k}
.

This proves the claim.

Define
H_{n+1}\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}{H_{n}}[{{{pt}_{k}}\mapsto{{new}_{k}}}]_{k\in\overline{u}}
.
Hence,
H_{n}\xrightarrow{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})\ \left\langle true\right\rangle}H_{n+1}
is a valid
step corresponding to a successful
mcas
step.

In case
x_{n+1}\in T
, Axiom
C_{\ref{help-focused::fin-predicate}}

also concludes that
true=v={x_{n+1}}.\textit{out}
