.

(b)

Otherwise,
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\>\widehat{=}\>}(\emptyset,\bot)
.

We argue that
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
is well-defined.
For that matter, we need to show that the
function’s conditions pick spans uniquely.

First, let us focus on the
mcas
cases. Notice that whenever
an
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
invocation starts, a fresh pointer

d
is created at Line
32

to store the descriptor
desc
.
This pointer
d
will serve as unique identifier for
the invocation, because the invoking thread executes
line
32
only once.

The invoking thread then enters the
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}

procedure and tries to write
d
into every pointer in
\overline{u}

by executing
writeall
.
Procedure
writeall
will try every pointer in the order of
\overline{u}
.

If for some thread
T
and

j\in\overline{u}
, the
rdcss
at line
51

returns a non-expected value and the output is not
a descriptor, and then later
T
manages to set the descriptor status
to
Fail
at line
40
, then this will correspond to case
(1).(b) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
. Notice
that for every
k<j
, the descriptor must have been written
at line
51
, because pointers were tried in the order of
\overline{u}

before it failed for
j
. Also, any
rdcss
at line
51

that returns the expected value and
executes before the descriptor status is set, must
have succeeded because the descriptor status must be
Undec

before the descriptor status is changed from
Undec

to
Succ
or
Fail
at line
40
.
Also, notice that for case (1).(b) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
,
rep events
i_{s}
,
i_{a}
, and
r
are chosen
uniquely because they execute at most once
(for rep event
r
, this is so
because the descriptor status starts from
Undec
at line
30
and
the status can only change from

Undec
into either
Succ
or
Fail
at line
40
),
while the
a_{k}
’s and the
a_{j}
are chosen uniquely
because they are the most recent
rdcss
rep events
of the appropriate kind before
r
.

If on the other hand, for every
j\in\overline{u}
,
the
rdcss
at line
51
returns the expected value and later
some thread manages to set the descriptor status
to
Succ
at line
40
, then this will correspond to case
(1).(a) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
.
The reasoning for the unique choosing of the rep events is identical
as in the (1).(b) case.

For the cases
x=\textit{mread}(p)
and

x=\textit{mwrite}(p,v)
,
their condition is choosing instructions at Lines

10
and

23

such that their output do not satisfy

is_mdesc
(by definition of set
\mathcal{S}_{p}
).
But this condition can happen at most once during the
invocation of
x
,
because the invoking thread finishes
x
once
the condition is satisfied.

While the case for

x=\textit{malloc}(v)
is trivial, since the
rep alloc executes at most once within the invocation of

x
.

We are ready to prove that

V^{\textsc{M}}(\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}},\mathrel{\ltimes}_{{p}})
is implemented by span
structure
S^{\textsc{M}}
, i.e., we are going to prove that
the span axioms of Figure
12
are satisfied. We require a couple of lemmas.

Lemma C.17
.

If
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
are allocs at line
28

such that they allocate the same pointer, then
i_{1}=i_{2}
.

Proof.

Allocs create fresh pointers, and pointers are not deallocated.
Therefore, if
{i_{1}}<^{R}{i_{2}}
, then
i_{2}
must create
a pointer that is different from the one created by
i_{1}
(Contradicting
the hypothesis).
Similarly, if
{i_{2}}<^{R}{i_{1}}
.
Hence,
i_{1}=i_{2}
.
∎

For the next lemma we make the following definition,

Definition C.3
.

We say that
v\in\textsc{Val}
is the value that
p
is bound to by
(a,\_)\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}

if there are
rD
,
desc
,
j
such that,

•

a=\textit{rdcss}(rD)
,

•

{rD}.new_{2}
was allocated by rep event
\textit{ralloc}(desc,\textsc{dptr})
at line

32
,

•

j\in{desc}.\textit{entries}
,

•

p={rD}.pt_{2}={pt}_{j}
,

•

v={new}_{j}
.

Alternatively, one can think of
v
as the new value that
line
44
will write into
p

because span
(a,\_)
has already resolved the descriptor status to
Succ
.

Lemma C.18
.

If
b\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
,
then there is
x
such that
b\in\mathcal{S}(x)
and
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {p}\ {v}
,
where
v
is the value determined by one of the following cases:

•

If
b
is of the form
(i)
for some rep
i
, then
v
is the value written by
i
.

•

If
b
is of the form
(a,\_)
for some rep
a
, then

v
is the value that
p
is bound to by
b
.

Proof.

Each span in
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
occurs in one of the cases in
the definition of
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
.

For example, consider the span of the form
(a,e)
at entry 2 in the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{p}
.
Reps
a
and
e
being instances of the code, must have the forms
a=\textit{rdcss}(rD)
,

e=\textit{rCAS}({desc}.\textit{status},\textsc{Undec},\textsc{Succ})

for some
rD
and
desc
such that
{rD}.new_{2}
was allocated by some rep
event
c=\textit{ralloc}(desc,\textsc{dptr})
at line

32
such that
{rD}.new_{2}={c}.\textit{out}
.
In this case,
p={rD}.pt_{2}={pt}_{j}
and
{a}.\textit{out}={rD}.exp_{2}={exp}_{j}

for some
j\in{desc}.\textit{entries}
.

In other words, the status pointer was set to
Succ
by some thread
T
, which means that

T
must have tried to write the descriptor at every input pointer and
T
either succeeded
in doing so or found out some other thread(s) did it already (line
39
).
Since
a
writes into pointer
{pt}_{j}
, and all the entries in

{desc}.\textit{entries}
wrote the descriptor in their respective pointers,

(a,b)
will be part of the successful case (1).(a) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{x}\right\rrbracket
for

x=\textit{mcas}
.
Also, notice that by definition,
\textsc{writer}\ {x}\ {{pt}_{j}}\ {{new}_{j}}
.

The rest of spans in
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
are even simpler, because
they are 1-tuple spans of the form
(b)
, and
b
is executed by the same thread
that invoked the exportable procedure that
b
is part of.
∎

Lemma C.19
.

Suppose
x
is one of
mcas
, or
mread
. Suppose
p\in\textsc{pts}
and
v\in\textsc{Val}
. Then,
the following statements are equivalent,

•

There is
y
such that
{y}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{x}

and
\textsc{writer}\ {y}\ {p}\ {v}
.

•

There is
b\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(x)
such that
f({b})
reads value
v
.

Proof.

We prove each direction.

\Longrightarrow
. By definition of
{y}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{x}
,
there are
b_{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(y)
,

b_{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(x)
such that
b_{y}
is the most recent span under
\sqsubseteq^{S}

satisfying
b_{y}\in\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
and
{b_{y}}\sqsubset^{S}{b_{x}}
.

The possible spans for
b_{x}
in the denotation of
x=\textit{mcas}
or
x=\textit{mread}
, they all
read a non-descriptor value in
f({b_{x}})
. This means that if
b_{y}
is of the
form
(a,b)
, line
44
must have replaced the descriptor with a
Val
value after
b
executed, otherwise
the descriptor would still be in the pointer and
f({b_{x}})
would read a descriptor.
Additionally, line
44
must have written
v
, since
v
is the value that
y
is going to write into
p

in case it succeeds (by definition of
writer
), which it does, since
b_{y}=(a,b)
is a successful
p
-write span,
which means it must belong to the (1).(a) case in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
(see proof of Lemma

C.18
).
If
b_{y}
has the form
(i)
, then
i
must write
v
because
i
is a successful write,
which means that
(i)
must belong to the (3).(a) or (4).(a) cases in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
.
Therefore, we can assume that there is a rep event
i
that writes
v
, such that

l({b_{y}})\leq^{R}i<^{R}f({b_{x}})
and
that there is no rep
p
-write in between
l({b_{y}})
and
i
.

We argue that any
p
-mutating rep event in between
i
and
f({b_{x}})
must eventually
restore the value to
v
before
f({b_{x}})
reads
p
.

If the
p
-write rep event corresponds to line
23
, it would
be a 1-span, contradicting that there are no
p
-writing spans in between

b_{y}
and
b_{x}
. So, let us focus on rep events at lines
40
,

44
, and
51
.

If the
rdcss
at line
51
returns the expected value
and executes before the descriptor status is set at line
40
,
then the
rdcss
succeeded in writing a descriptor, up to the point where the
descriptor is removed at line
44
. Therefore

f({b_{x}})
must occur after the rep event at line
44
, otherwise

f({b_{x}})
would read a descriptor. However, the resolution
at line
40
cannot be
Succ
, since this would
contradict that there are no
p
-write spans in between
b_{x}
and
b_{y}
. Since
the resolution must be
Fail
, the removal of the descriptor at line

44
will restore the pointers to the values they had before the
descriptor was written.
In other words,
v
is preserved and
f({b_{x}})
reads
v
.

Notice that we can ignore the failing cases of
rdcss
at line
51
:
when
rdcss
returns a non-expected value
or when
rdcss
does return the expected value but executes after the descriptor status is set at line
40
,
since the status is no longer
Undec
.

\Longleftarrow
. Let
b_{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{p}(x)

such that
f({b_{x}})
reads value
v\in\textsc{Val}
.
Therefore, there is a most recent rep event
w
that wrote
v
into
p
.
If
w
corresponds to line
44

and it is removing a descriptor that was set as
Fail
by a rep event
r
at line

40
, then
r
is the last rep event in a span that failed
to modify the pointer, which means that
w
is restoring the pointer
to the value it had before the descriptor was written at line
51
.
If this is the case, we just keep searching for a
p
-write rep event of
v

that does not correspond to line
44
having a descriptor resolution of
Fail

at line
40
.
This rep event exists because there is an alloc of
p
as first rep event for
p
.

Suppose
w^{\prime}
is the chosen
p
-write event. If
w^{\prime}
corresponds to line
44

then there will be a previous rep event
r^{\prime}
resolving the status to
Succ
at line
40
.
We will have
r^{\prime}=l({b})
for some span
b
in
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}
,
since the set
\mathcal{S}_{p}
covers all possible places where a successful resolution could occur
in the code.
If
w^{\prime}
does not correspond to line
44

then
w^{\prime}=l({b})
for some span
b
in
\mathcal{S}^{W}_{p}

since
w^{\prime}
can only be either line
23
or
line
28
.

Span
b
will be the most recent
p
-write span
before
b_{x}
, since all the more recent spans we found correspond to failing spans.
By Lemma
C.18
,
there is
y
such that
b\in\mathcal{S}(y)
and
\textsc{writer}\ {y}\ {p}\ {v}
.
Therefore,
{y}\mathrel{\lessdot}_{{p}}{x}

by definition.
∎

Lemma C.20
.

Axiom
S_{\ref{principle::descriptors-do-not-interfere}}
holds.

Proof.

Since this is trivial when
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are 1-tuple spans,
we only need to consider the cases
when
b_{1}
is a 1-span and
b_{2}
a 2-tuple span or when both

b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are 2-tuple spans.

•

Case
b_{1}=(c)
is a 1-tuple span and
b_{2}=(e,g)
a 2-tuple span.

Since every procedure invocation requires that input pointers be previously
allocated, there must be some rep alloc
i
such that

{i}<^{R}{e}
. We also know that there cannot be a
p
-writer rep in
between
e
and
g
, because once a descriptor is written by
e
, no other write can
occur up to the point where the descriptor is resolved by
g
(and even until the
descriptor is removed at line
44
which is not part of the span).

If
c
is an alloc, by Lemma
C.1
,

c=i
and
{b_{1}}\sqsubset^{S}{b_{2}}
follows. So, we can assume that
c
is not an alloc.

By going through all the cases for
(c)\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
,
we see that all the cases imply
\neg\textit{is\_rdesc}({c}.\textit{out})
,
i.e.,
c
does not read a descriptor.

Therefore,
c
cannot occur in between
e
and
g
, because
e
writes a descriptor.
Also, the cases
c=e
or
c=g
are excluded because
\mathcal{S}_{p}

is defined so that 1-spans and 2-tuple spans do not share rep events.

•

Case
b_{1}=(c,d)
is a 2-tuple span and
b_{2}=(e,g)
a 2-tuple span.

By definition of
\mathcal{S}_{p}
, we know there is no writer into
p

in between
c
and
d
and in between
e
and
g
. But
c
and
e

are writers into
p
by definition of
\mathcal{S}_{p}
.
Therefore, we must have
{e}<^{R}{c}
, or

e=c
, or
{d}<^{R}{e}
(the case
d=e
is impossible, because

d
and
e
correspond to different code lines:
e
writes a descriptor, while

d
resolves resolves a descriptor).

–

Case
{d}<^{R}{e}
. Hence
{b_{1}}\sqsubset^{S}{b_{2}}
.

–

Case
{e}<^{R}{c}
. We have either
{g}<^{R}{c}
, or

c<^{R}g<^{R}d
, or

g=d
, or
{d}<^{R}{g}
(the case
g=c
is impossible,
because
g
resolves a descriptor, while
c
writes one).

The cases
g=d
and
{d}<^{R}{g}
would lead to

e<^{R}c<^{R}g
(Contradiction).
If
c<^{R}g<^{R}d
,
then we also have
e<^{R}c<^{R}g
,
since
{e}<^{R}{c}
(Contradiction).
Therefore,
{g}<^{R}{c}
and
{b_{2}}\sqsubset^{S}{b_{1}}
follows.

–

Case
e=c
. We have either

c<^{R}g<^{R}d
, or

g=d
, or
{d}<^{R}{g}
(the case
{g}<^{R}{c}
is
impossible because
g
occurs after
e=c
).
Since
e=c
, it means that
e
and
c
wrote the same descriptor.

The case
e=c<^{R}g<^{R}d

means that the descriptor status was resolved twice, which is impossible (Contradiction).

The case
{d}<^{R}{g}
leads to

c=e<^{R}d<^{R}g
, which means that
the descriptor was again resolved twice (Contradiction).

Therefore
g=d
also holds and
b_{1}=b_{2}
follows.

∎

Lemma C.21
.

Axiom
S_{\ref{principle::all-descriptors-are-written-before-any-resolution}}
holds.

Proof.

This is trivial when
x
has a denotation with only one span.
So, it is enough to check the cases for
x=\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.

The failing denotation
has the form
\{(a_{k},r),\ (c_{j})\mid k\in\overline{u}\wedge k<j\}
for some
j\in\overline{u}
,
where
c_{j}
occurs after all the
a_{k}
’s since
c_{j}
is the last
rdcss
that fails
at line
51
. Also,
c_{j}
occurs before
r
, because
the failing
rdcss
at line
51
precedes the descriptor status change
at line
40
.
Therefore
(c_{j})
overlaps with all the
(a_{k},r)
, while any two
(a_{k},r)
overlap because
they share
r
.

The success denotation has the form
\{(a_{j},r)\mid j\in\overline{u}\}
. Any two
(a_{j},r)
overlap because
they share
r
.
∎

Lemma C.22
.

Axioms

S_{\ref{principle::finished-operations-have-a-run}}
holds.

Proof.

We prove each procedure in turn.

•

Case
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
.
Suppose that
\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})
finished.
Then, the invoking thread
T
must have created a pointer
d
to the
descriptor at line
32
, and finished the invocation of

\textit{mcas}^{\prime}(d)
at line
33
.
Thread
T
must have reached line
45
, which returns a
boolean
r
.

–

Case
r=true
.
T
must have read
d
’s status pointer to be
Succ

at line
41
. This implies that some thread
T^{\prime}
(which could be
T
as well)
must have set the status pointer to
Succ
at line
40
and
T^{\prime}
must
have finished the execution of
writeall
with a
Succ
at line
39
.
Since
writeall
is a recursive method which only recurses when it needs to try again, and we know that
T^{\prime}

finished executing
writeall
,
T^{\prime}
must have reached
an invocation of
writeall
that did not recurse, i.e., an
invocation of
writeall
where
lines
54
-
55
were not executed. So, we
can assume we are on such execution.

Since
writeall
returned
Succ
,
thread
T^{\prime}
must have tried all the entries in the loop.
For each entry
i\in\overline{u}
,
T^{\prime}
must have tried to write
d
using the

rdcss
at line
51
.
The
rdcss
must have returned either
d
or the expected
value
{exp}_{i}
(it cannot return a descriptor different
from
d
, because lines
54
-
55
would be executed).
If the returned
value was the expected value
{exp}_{i}
, then the
rdcss
succeeded (since we
already know that the status was set to
Succ
by
T^{\prime}
later and the
status is
Undec
previous to the change). If the
rdcss
at line
51

returned
d
, then some other thread (or
T^{\prime}
in a previous recursive

writeall
invocation) already wrote the descriptor. In other words,
in both cases the descriptor was written. Since we know that
d
was
written for each entry and its status pointer was set to
Succ

later, this corresponds to case (1).(a) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\right\rrbracket
,
which has as denotation output
true
and the denotation is not empty.

–

Case
r=false
.
Notice that no thread can reach line
44
unless some thread reaches
line
40
first and changes the status to either

Succ
or
Fail
. The
reason is that while the status is
Undec
, threads starting
\textit{mcas}^{\prime}

will enter the true case of the conditional at line
38
.

Having said this,
T
must have read the status pointer of
d
to be
different from
Succ
at line
41
. But since no thread can reach
line
44
while the status is
Undec
,
T
must have read
Fail
at line
41
.
This implies that some thread
T^{\prime}
(which could be
T
as well)
must have set the status pointer to
Fail
at line
40
and
T^{\prime}
must
have finished the execution of
writeall
with a
Fail
at
line
39
. Since
writeall
is a recursive method that recurses only to try again,
and we know that
T^{\prime}
finished executing
writeall
, thread
T^{\prime}
must have reached
an invocation of
writeall
that did not recurse, i.e., an
invocation of
writeall
where lines
54
-
55
were not executed.
So, we can assume we are on such execution. Since
writeall
returned
Fail
,
thread
T^{\prime}
must have tried a last entry
l
in the loop for
which the
rdcss
at line
51
returned a value different from the
expected value (otherwise
T^{\prime}
could not have reached line
57
),
meaning that the
rdcss
failed for entry
l
. But this means that
for all entries
i<l
, the
rdcss
must have returned
either
d
or the expected value
{exp}_{i}
(it cannot return
a descriptor different from
d
, because lines
54
-
55
would be
executed), otherwise the loop would have been interrupted before
entry
l
. For entry
i
, if the returned value at line
51
was the
expected value, then the
rdcss
succeeded (since we already know
that the status was set to
Fail
by
T^{\prime}
later and the status is

Undec
previous to the change). For entry
i
, if the
rdcss
at
line
51
returned
d
, then some other thread (or
T^{\prime}
in a previous
recursive
writeall
invocation) already wrote the descriptor. In
other words, in both cases the descriptor was written for every
entry before
l
. Since we know that
d
was written for each entry
before
l
, and the
rdcss
at line
51
failed for
T^{\prime}
at entry
l

and the status pointer was set to
Fail
by
T^{\prime}
later, this
corresponds to case (1).(b) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\textit{mcas}(\overline{u})}\right\rrbracket
, which
has as denotation output
false
and the denotation is not empty.

•

Case
\textit{mread}(p)
. Suppose that
\textit{mread}(p)
finished.
Let
T
be the invoking thread. Since
mread
is a recursive method and
we know that
T
finished executing it, this means that
T
must have
reached an invocation of
mread
that did not recurse, i.e., an
invocation where lines

12
-
13
were not executed.
So, we can assume we
are on such execution. Since
T
reached line
15
,
the read at line
10

must have returned a non-descriptor value
v
. This corresponds to
(2).(a) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
, with denotation output
whatever line
10
produced (which is equal to
v
)
and the denotation is not empty.

•

Case
\textit{mwrite}(p,v)
. Suppose that
\textit{mwrite}(p,v)
finished.
Let
T
be the invoking thread. Since
mwrite
is a recursive method and
we know that
T
finished executing it, this means that
T
must have
reached an invocation of
mwrite
that did not recurse, i.e., an
invocation where lines

20
-
21
,

25
were not executed. So, we can assume
we are on such execution. Since
T
must have returned after reaching
line
26
without entering the conditional
at line
24
, the CAS at line
23
must
have returned
old
(meaning that the CAS succeeded), and at the same
time, the read at line
18
must have returned
old
, and
old
is a
non-descriptor value. Since the CAS at line
23
succeeded, this
corresponds to (3).(a) in the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
, with
denotation output
tt
and the denotation is not empty.

•

Case
\textit{malloc}(v)
. Suppose that
\textit{malloc}(v)
finished.
Let
T
be the invoking thread. Then
T
must have executed line
28

producing some pointer
p
as output. This corresponds to (4).(a) in
the definition of
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
with output whatever line
28

produced and the denotation is not empty.

∎

Lemma C.23
.

Axiom
S_{\ref{principle::descriptor-write-precedes-resolution}}
holds.

Proof.

Trivial for 1-spans. For 2-tuple spans
(a,b)
, the code can only resolve descriptors (rep event
b
)
only if it was previously written at line
51
(rep event
a
).
∎

Lemma C.24
.

.
Axiom

S_{\ref{principle::runs-are-injective}}
holds.

Proof.

First, let us focus when neither
x
nor
y
are
mcas
events.

If
b\in\mathcal{S}(x)\cap\mathcal{S}(y)
, then (by going through all the
non-
mcas
cases in
\left\llbracket{\cdot}\right\rrbracket
),

b=(i_{1})
for some
i_{1}
that is executed by the thread that invoked
x
and

b=(i_{2})
for some
i_{2}
that is executed by the thread that invoked
y
.
In addition,
i_{1}
occurs within the invocation of
x
and
i_{2}
within
the invocation of
y
. Therefore,
i_{1}=i_{2}
, which means that
x
and
y

are invoked by the same thread.

If
x\neq y
, then
x
and
y
cannot overlap in real-time, because they
are invoked by the same thread. But this contradicts that
i_{1}
occurs within
the invocation of both
x
and
y
. Therefore,
x=y
.

Now, let us check the case when either
x
is an
mcas
event or
y
is.
Say,
x
is an
mcas
event.

All the spans in the cases for
mcas
satisfy that
f({b})
has code line

51
. This means that
f({b})

has the form
\textit{rdcss}(rD)
such that
{rD}.new_{2}=d
, where
d
is the unique
identifier for the invocation
x
generated at line

32
(see discussion that the denotation
is well-defined). Since
b
also occurs in the denotation of
y
and
d
is unique
per-invocation, it must be the case that
x=y
.
∎

Lemma C.25
.

Axiom

S_{\ref{principle::writer-blocks-belong-to-runs}}
holds.

Proof.

Directly from Lemma
C.18
.
